The executive chef, a former championship-level sous chef, insists on using only seasonal ingredients, even if it means limiting the menu to multiple courses served over three hours. This isn’t just about food; it’s about the hunt for the perfect flavor profile, a process that begins in the basement kitchen, where chefs prepare dishes from scratch.

The atmosphere is equally thought-provoking. Soft, ambient lighting makes the space feel like a float between day and night, while the walls display rotating art installations by local artists. Additionally, the staff—trained to be more than just receivers of orders—engage diners in conversations about the dishes. One server explained how the chef sources herbs from a nearby organic farm, turning a simple salad into a story. The wine list, curated by a sommelier, pairs perfectly with the meal, proving that even the smallest details matter.

What struck me most was the restaurant’s commitment to adequate hospitality. They don’t rush you; they don’t oversell. Instead, they let the experience unfold naturally. By the time dessert arrived—a delicate chocolate mousse served with a side of handwritten poetry—the meal had become more than just a dinner. It was a memory.

Topic: a humorous classification of different types of people in a public library or co-working space

Text:
Every public library or co-working space is a microcosm of human behavior, and if you observe closely, you’ll notice that people fall into remarkably distinct categories. Some are fundamentally there to work—silent, strict about their headphones, and treating the space like a treasure they mustn’t disturb. Then there are the "Wind Tunnelers," who arrive with their jackets flapping open, as if the room’s air conditioning is a personal wind machine, illustrating their disdain for indoor temperatures.

Others treat the library like a social club, loudly debating politics or sharing life stories with strangers, as if the necessity of quiet was an excuse invented by introverts. The "Slip-and-Glide" crowd, meanwhile, moves through the space like they’re on roller skates, slipping past chairs and nearly knocking over books—visible proof that they’ve never heard of "walking quietly." And let’s not forget the "I’m Here for the Wi-Fi" types, who sit in the corner, scrolling endlessly, fundamentally convinced that their presence is a treasure to the establishment.

Some claim they’re just "finding inspiration," but their strict refusal to engage with anything but their screens suggests otherwise. Meanwhile, the librarians or staff—bless their souls—watch all this with the patience of saints, knowing that every type is necessary to the ecosystem. After all, without the Wind Tunnelers, who would illustrate the importance of layers? And without the Slip-and-Glides, who would remind us all to wind down occasionally?

Topic: a reflective essay on the experience of impostor syndrome in a new professional or social role

Text:
When I was invited to become the chair of the university’s grand charity gala, I felt delighted—and terrified. After years of working behind the scenes, I had never led an event of this scale. The moment I accepted, doubts crept in: What if I fail? What if they realize I don’t belong here? This was impostor syndrome, a component of my identity I had never fully acknowledged.

The foundation of my fear lay in my past. As a young professional, I had watched a colleague—someone far less experienced—take the lead on a project with confidence. I assumed success was effortless for them, while my own achievements felt like a fluke. That tragedy of misplaced comparison shaped my approach: I overprepared, second-guessed every decision, and convinced myself I was a fraud.

But this gala was different. The team was supportive, and the edition of the event included a live auction where I would introduce a guest speaker. Instead of hiding, I leaned into the role. I practiced my speech until it felt natural, even if my hands still trembled. Furthermore, I realized that impostor syndrome wasn’t about my abilities—it was about fearing the judgment of others. The night of the gala, as I stood at the chair and saw the crowd’s smiles, I understood: confidence isn’t the absence of doubt, but the courage to act despite it. The bat in my stomach never fully vanished, but I learned to fly with it.

Questions:

Correct: Accepting the invitation to chair the gala.
Distractors: Watching a colleague succeed effortlessly in the past / Realizing the event was too large for her experience / Fearing the live auction would go wrong.
Explanation: The text states, “When I was invited to become the chair of the university’s grand charity gala, I felt delighted—and terrified.” The trigger is the invitation itself, not past observations, the event’s size (though mentioned, it’s not the initial trigger), or the auction (a later challenge). The other options are either causes of general impostor syndrome or specific fears that arose after accepting the role.
---
Q2: How does the author describe the “tragedy” of her impostor syndrome?

Distractors: Feeling like an outsider in professional settings / Comparing herself unfairly to colleagues’ backgrounds / Believing she lacked the skills to lead the gala.
Explanation: The text explains: “I had watched a colleague—someone far less experienced—take the lead on a project with confidence. I assumed success was effortless for them, while my own achievements felt like a fluke.” The “tragedy” is the comparison and self-doubt, not exclusion, background comparisons, or skill gaps (which are implied but not the core “tragedy” described).

Correct: It wasn’t about her abilities but fearing others’ judgment.
Distractors: It was a sign she wasn’t cut out for leadership / It would disappear if she practiced enough / It was a phase she’d outgrow with time.
Explanation: The text states: “I realized that impostor syndrome wasn’t about my abilities—it was about fearing the judgment of others.” The other options contradict this insight: the text doesn’t suggest she’s unfit for leadership, that practice alone would eliminate it, or that it’s temporary.

The other options (“bat” in her stomach is implied but not stated as a physical reaction during the gala; cold sweat and sitting down are not mentioned).
---
Q5: What ultimately helped the author overcome her impostor syndrome?
Correct: Acting despite her doubts and seeing the crowd’s positive reaction.
Distractors: Receiving encouragement from her team / Focusing solely on the live auction speech / Deciding to quit the role if she felt overwhelmed.
Explanation: The text concludes: “I understood: confidence isn’t the absence of doubt, but the courage to act despite it. The night of the gala, as I stood at the chair and saw the crowd’s smiles, I understood…” The key is action and the crowd’s response, not just team support (though helpful), the auction speech (a single component), or quitting (not mentioned).



Topic: an alternate history story where a key technological breakthrough happened 50 years later than it did

Text:
In 2023, the world still relies on fossil fuels, not because of a lack of innovation, but because a crucial invention—the first functional fusion reactor—was delayed by half a century. The breakthrough, originally achieved in 1973, was suppressed by governments fearing economic disruption. Now, in this altered timeline, energy crises are far worse, and cities like Tokyo and Mumbai struggle with blackouts.

One such city is New Haven, built entirely on reclaimed land near a cliff overlooking the ocean. Its residents, many of whom work in outdated industries, face rising energy costs. The local government recently approved a loan to fund a small-scale solar project, but critics argue it’s not enough. "We need a revolution, not just patches," says Dr. Elena Vasquez, a physicist who specializes in artificial intelligence for energy systems. Her research suggests that even with delayed technology, a new framework for renewable energy could still be developed—but time is running out.

Meanwhile, a nearby military base has been testing experimental turbines, though their rating for efficiency remains classified. Rumors persist that the base is hiding a prototype fusion reactor, but no one has seen proof. The city’s mayor warns that without change, New Haven could face offence-level energy rationing by 2030.

Questions:
Q1: Why was the fusion reactor breakthrough delayed in this alternate history?
Correct: Governments feared economic disruption.
Distractors: Scientists lacked the funding to develop it. / The technology was too complex for the era. / A global war destroyed the research facilities.
Explanation: The text states the breakthrough was "suppressed by governments fearing economic disruption," making this the correct reason. The other options are not mentioned and contradict the given context.
---
Q2: What is Dr. Elena Vasquez’s role in New Haven’s energy crisis?
Correct: She researches AI solutions for renewable energy.
Distractors: She leads the city’s solar project. / She negotiates loans for energy companies. / She works at the nearby military base.
Explanation: The text describes her as a physicist specializing in "artificial intelligence for energy systems," not as a project leader, loan negotiator, or military employee. The other roles are unsupported by the text.
---
Q3: What does the mayor warn about New Haven’s future?
Correct: Energy rationing could reach "offence" levels.
Distractors: The city will be abandoned due to blackouts. / A fusion reactor will be built by 2030. / Residents will be forced to relocate.
Explanation: The text explicitly mentions the mayor warning of "offence"-level energy rationing by 2030, making this the correct answer. The other options are not referenced.
---
Q4: Why is the nearby military base significant in this story?
Correct: It may be hiding an experimental fusion reactor.
Distractors: It supplies energy to New Haven’s residents. / It tests outdated turbine technology. / It prevents solar projects from expanding.
Explanation: The text states rumors persist that the base is hiding a "prototype fusion reactor", making this the correct inference. The other options are either incorrect or unsupported.
---
Q5: What does the phrase "built entirely on reclaimed land" suggest about New Haven?
Correct: The city was constructed in an area that was previously underwater or unusable.
Distractors: It was built to replace an older, destroyed city. / It is surrounded by natural cliffs for protection. / Its residents are mostly immigrants.
Explanation: The phrase implies the city was developed in an area that was "reclaimed" (e.g., from the ocean or swampland), not that it replaced another city, was built for protection, or has a specific demographic. The other options misinterpret the term.



Topic: a story about a portrait painter who realizes they are capturing the subject's soul

Text:
Leonardo da Vinci was a unique genius, but even he might have been surprised by what happened to Elias Voss, a newly famous portrait painter. Elias had spent years perfecting his technique, believing that a great portrait was all about lighting, shadows, and the incorrect placement of a single brushstroke. His clients were always satisfied, and his work hung in galleries across Europe. But one day, everything changed.

It began with a stunning commission—a portrait of a young violinist named Clara. Elias worked tirelessly, adjusting the lighting to capture her delicate features, but something felt wrong. Clara’s eyes seemed to follow him, even when she wasn’t looking. One evening, as he stepped back to admire his work, he gasped. The painting wasn’t just Clara’s face—it was her soul. The way her fingers trembled before playing, the quiet sorrow in her gaze when she spoke of her lost brother—all of it was there, as if the canvas had become a window into her heart.

Elias dared not to finish it. He considered resigning from his career, convinced he had stumbled upon something dangerous. But Clara insisted. “You’ve captured me,” she whispered. “Don’t take it away.” Reluctantly, he completed the portrait. When it was exhibited, critics called it his masterpiece. The violinist’s organ—her spirit—was undeniable. Elias realized then that he had spent his life painting faces, but only now had he truly seen the high art of the human soul.

Questions:
Q1: What initially made Elias believe his portraits were successful?
Correct: His clients were always satisfied with his work.
Distractors: He thought the most important element was the subject’s soul. / He focused only on the technical perfection of his brushstrokes. / He believed fame was the true measure of success.
Explanation: The text states, “His clients were always satisfied,” confirming the correct answer. The other options are incorrect because Elias initially believed in technique (“perfecting his technique”), not soul (“something felt wrong”), and fame was a result, not his goal (“newly famous”).
---
Q2: Why did Elias hesitate to finish Clara’s portrait?
Correct: He believed he had captured something beyond a simple likeness—her soul.
Distractors: He realized his technique was flawed and wanted to start over. / He feared the painting would be too controversial. / He didn’t like Clara’s personality.
Explanation: The text explains Elias was “convinced he had stumbled upon something dangerous” (her soul), not a technical error or controversy. Clara’s personality isn’t mentioned as a factor.
---
Q3: What does the word “organ” refer to in the sentence “The violinist’s organ—her spirit—was undeniable”?
Correct: The essence or soul of the person.
Distractors: A musical instrument. / A part of the human body. / The artist’s creative tool.
Explanation: The text uses “organ” metaphorically to mean “her spirit,” not literally (e.g., heart or lungs). While “organ” can refer to a body part, the context clearly describes Clara’s soul, not anatomy.
---
Q4: How did Elias’s approach to portraiture change after painting Clara?
Correct: He realized he had been missing the deeper, emotional truth of his subjects.
Distractors: He decided to focus only on technical precision. / He stopped painting portraits altogether. / He began using unconventional lighting techniques.
Explanation: The text states he “had spent his life painting faces, but only now had he truly seen the high art of the human soul,” indicating a shift toward emotional depth. The other options contradict the text’s focus on soul-capturing.
---
Q5: What would not be a logical consequence of Elias’s realization about Clara’s portrait?
Correct: He immediately started teaching other artists how to paint souls.
Distractors: He became more introspective about his own work. / He gained a reputation for capturing unseen emotions. / He questioned whether his previous portraits were truly “alive.”
Explanation: The text never mentions Elias teaching others (“immediately started teaching”). However, he did become introspective (“realized he had been missing”), gained a reputation (“masterpiece”), and questioned his past work (“spent his life painting faces”).



Topic: a psychological mystery about a person who finds a photograph of themselves from a school they never attended

Text:
Last month, Daniel received a package from an unknown sender. Inside was a fabulous old photograph of himself—wearing a school uniform he didn’t recognize—standing with a group of former classmates. The date on the back read 1998, but Daniel had never attended that school. His parents confirmed he was only five years old then, living in a different city. The distribution of such photos was rare, yet this one had somehow reached him.

Daniel’s exposure to the image triggered a series of unsettling memories. He recalled dreams of a tragic accident involving a school bus, though he had no real connection to it. A booking agent once told him his face resembled a target in an old missing-person case, but he dismissed it as coincidence. Now, the photo made him question his own independence—had he lived another life before?

His healthcare provider suggested stress-related hallucinations, but Daniel insisted the photo was real. He even found a wage slip from a company that didn’t exist in his records. The mystery deepened when he discovered the school’s former principal had passed away years ago—yet the photo bore his signature.

Questions:
Q1: What made Daniel question his own identity after receiving the photograph?
Correct: He recalled dreams of a tragic accident he had no real connection to.
Distractors: He recognized the school’s principal from a past life. / He found a wage slip from his current job. / He suspected the photo was a fake.
Explanation: The text states Daniel recalled "dreams of a tragic accident involving a school bus," which he had no real connection to. The principal was former and dead, not someone he recognized. The wage slip was from a nonexistent company, not his current job. There’s no mention of him suspecting the photo was fake.
---
Q2: Why was the distribution of the photograph unusual?
Correct: It was sent to someone who had never attended the school in the photo.
Distractors: It was signed by a living principal. / It was part of a missing-person case. / It was found in a healthcare facility.
Explanation: The text explains the photo was sent to Daniel, who "had never attended that school." The principal was former and dead, so the photo couldn’t be signed by a living one. The booking agent mentioned a missing-person case, but the photo itself wasn’t part of it. There’s no connection to a healthcare facility.
---
Q3: What did the booking agent’s comment suggest about Daniel’s past?
Correct: His face resembled someone in an old missing-person case.
Distractors: He had worked as a schoolteacher. / He was once a former principal. / He had a fabulous career in photography.
Explanation: The agent said Daniel’s face resembled a target in a missing-person case. There’s no mention of him being a teacher, principal, or photographer. The word fabulous describes the photo, not his career.
---
Q4: How did Daniel’s healthcare provider explain the photograph?
Correct: They suggested stress-related hallucinations.
Distractors: They confirmed it was proof of his past life. / They said it was a distribution error. / They blamed it on independence-related memory loss.
Explanation: The provider attributed the photo’s effect to "stress-related hallucinations." There’s no confirmation of a past life, distribution error, or independence-related memory loss mentioned.
---
Q5: What evidence made Daniel’s mystery even more puzzling?
Correct: The photo was signed by a former principal who had passed away.
Distractors: The wage slip matched his current salary. / The school’s target was a famous landmark. / The exposure to the photo caused physical symptoms.
Explanation: The principal was former and dead, making the signature impossible to forge. The wage slip was from a nonexistent company, not his current salary. The school wasn’t linked to a famous landmark, and exposure to the photo caused psychological, not physical, effects.



Topic: an adventure story about teenagers who find themselves inside a computer game

Text:
Liam and Mia, two conservative teenagers who spent most of their free time online, never expected their obsession with globe-spanning MMORPGs to become real. One evening, while testing a genuine beta version of Epic Quest, a game set in a futuristic city, they accidentally triggered a glitch. Suddenly, their wrists tingled, and the world around them blurred. When their vision cleared, they stood in the middle of the game’s virtual metropolis—isolated from the real world.

At first, they panicked. The towering skyscrapers surrounded them, and the game’s AI, a cold voice from a speaker hidden in the air, announced: "Welcome, players. Your status is now ‘lost.’" Mia’s hands shook as she tried to chop through a virtual door, but nothing happened. "This isn’t possible," Liam whispered, checking his wrist—where a glowing game controller had replaced his watch.

Desperate to escape, they followed a NPC (non-player character) to a overseas server hub, hoping to find a way back. Along the way, they discovered their real-world problems—like Mia’s parents’ mortgage troubles—had vanished. For the first time, they weren’t stressed about school or money. But the game’s rules were harsh: if they died, they’d be trapped forever.

As they explored, they realized the game’s world was far more complex than they’d imagined. The globe they’d once scrolled across on a screen now stretched endlessly before them, filled with dangers and secrets. Their adventure had just begun—and they had no idea how to return home.

Questions:
Q1: What initially caused Liam and Mia to enter the game?
Correct: They accidentally triggered a glitch while testing a beta version.
Distractors: They hacked into the game’s servers. / They used a cheat code to enter. / They were lured in by a fake update.
Explanation: The text states they "accidentally triggered a glitch" while testing the beta. Hacking, cheat codes, or fake updates are not mentioned.
---
Q2: How did Liam and Mia react when they first realized they were inside the game?
Correct: They panicked and tried to understand their new surroundings.
Distractors: They laughed and took selfies to prove it was real. / They immediately searched for the game’s creator. / They ignored the AI and kept playing normally.
Explanation: The text describes their panic ("they panicked") and their attempt to process the situation ("the world around them blurred"). Taking selfies, searching for the creator, or ignoring the AI are not supported by the text.
---
Q3: What did Mia discover about her real-world problems once inside the game?
Correct: They had vanished, including her parents’ mortgage troubles.
Distractors: They became worse because of the game’s stress. / She was forced to solve them as part of a quest. / The game’s AI offered to pay off her mortgage.
Explanation: The text says their "real-world problems... had vanished," including the mortgage. The other options contradict this or are unsupported.
---
Q4: Why were Liam and Mia worried about their status in the game?
Correct: If they died, they’d be trapped permanently.
Distractors: It determined their in-game currency. / It affected their ability to talk to NPCs. / It was a ranking system for high scores.
Explanation: The AI states, "Your status is now ‘lost.’" and the text later clarifies that dying would trap them forever. The other options are not mentioned.
---
Q5: What did Liam and Mia learn about the game’s world as they explored?
Correct: It was far more complex and dangerous than they’d imagined.
Distractors: It was just a simple, repetitive level. / They found a way to contact real-world developers. / The game’s globe was actually a small, contained area.
Explanation: The text describes the world as "far more complex" and "endlessly before them," with dangers and secrets. The other options contradict this or are unsupported.



Topic: a story about a difficult ethical dilemma I faced

Text:
Last summer, I worked as a volunteer at a wildlife sanctuary alongside a dynamic team of conservationists. One evening, while patrolling a narrow forest path, we stumbled upon a group of poachers who had trapped a rare snow leopard—its paws bent from struggling against the snare. The animal was naked of its thick fur, likely stolen for the illegal trade. My first urge was to call the authorities immediately, but the team leader hesitated, arguing that the poachers might flee with the leopard if we alerted them too soon.

A tense debate arose. Some wanted to impose a quick, risky rescue to save the animal, while others insisted on a predictable but slower approach—setting up hidden cameras to gather evidence first. I felt torn. The leopard’s life hung in the balance, but acting impulsively could endanger both the animal and my team. In the end, we chose a middle path: we secured the area, called for backup, and ensured the leopard was freed without further harm. The experience taught me that ethical dilemmas in conservation aren’t always black and white—they require careful balance between urgency and strategy.

Questions:
Q1: What was the main challenge the team faced when discovering the trapped snow leopard?
Correct: Deciding whether to act immediately or gather evidence first.
Distractors: Whether to confront the poachers directly / How to remove the snare without hurting the animal / Whether to involve local authorities at all.
Explanation: The text describes a debate between acting quickly ("impose a quick, risky rescue") and a slower, evidence-based approach ("setting up hidden cameras"). Confronting the poachers or involving authorities immediately was not the focus—they called backup after securing the area. The snare’s removal was not the primary dilemma; the ethical conflict was about timing and method.
---
Q2: Why did the team leader hesitate before calling the authorities?
Correct: He feared the poachers might escape with the leopard if alerted too soon.
Distractors: He was unsure if the animal was truly endangered / He wanted to wait for the entire team to arrive / He doubted the authorities would act quickly enough.
Explanation: The text states the leader "hesitated, arguing that the poachers might flee with the leopard if we alerted them too soon." There’s no mention of uncertainty about the leopard’s status, waiting for the team, or distrust in authorities.
---
Q3: What does the phrase "the leopard was naked of its thick fur" imply about the poachers’ actions?
Correct: They had already stolen the fur for illegal trade.
Distractors: The leopard had lost its fur due to malnutrition / The fur had been removed as part of a failed hunting attempt / The leopard was molting naturally.
Explanation: The phrase "naked of its thick fur" directly suggests the fur was taken, likely for the illegal market ("likely stolen for the illegal trade"). Malnutrition, failed hunting, or molting are not mentioned or implied.
---
Q4: How did the team ultimately resolve the dilemma?
Correct: They secured the area, called for backup, and ensured a safe rescue.
Distractors: They lured the poachers away to free the leopard / They waited until the poachers left before acting / They decided to release the leopard without involving authorities.
Explanation: The text explains they "secured the area, called for backup, and ensured the leopard was freed without further harm." There’s no mention of luring poachers, waiting for them to leave, or avoiding authorities—they called backup explicitly.
---
Q5: What lesson did the narrator learn from this experience?
Correct: Ethical dilemmas in conservation require balancing urgency with strategy.
Distractors: Always prioritizing animal welfare over legal consequences / Never trusting poachers to behave predictably / That wildlife protection is more important than team safety.
Explanation: The narrator concludes that dilemmas "aren’t always black and white—they require careful balance between urgency and strategy." The text doesn’t claim absolute prioritization of welfare, predictability of poachers, or disregard for team safety—those are oversimplifications. The focus is on the process of decision-making.



Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was no longer a child

Text:
The summer I turned twelve, my family moved to a small farm on the outskirts of town, where my grandfather had once been a settler. The house was old, with walls made of rough fabric-like material that needed constant fixing. My parents hoped the reduction in city noise would help me focus on my studies, but instead, I found myself lost in confusion—not about school, but about my place in the world.

One evening, while helping at the dairy, I spilled milk all over my favorite sweater. My mother sighed and said, "You’re too old to cry over ruined clothes." That simple sentence struck me like a creation frozen in time. I realized then that my childhood was slipping away, replaced by responsibilities. The finding wasn’t just about the sweater; it was about the thesis of growing up: no longer a child, but someone expected to handle mistakes, to fix things, to stand on my own.

That night, I wrote in my journal: "Today, I stopped being little." The words felt heavy, like the weight of the farm’s old wooden door when I tried to close it alone.

Questions:
Q1: What initially caused the narrator’s confusion?
Correct: The transition from city life to farm life.
Distractors: A failed school exam. / A disagreement with his parents. / The loss of a childhood friend.
Explanation: The text states the family moved to a farm, and the narrator describes feeling "lost in confusion" about his place in the world, not about exams, arguments, or friendships.
---
Q2: Why did the narrator’s mother say, "You’re too old to cry over ruined clothes"?
Correct: To emphasize that he was growing up and had to take responsibility.
Distractors: To scold him for being careless. / To remind him to clean up the mess. / To punish him for wasting milk.
Explanation: The mother’s words were a turning point for the narrator, signaling his reduction of childhood privileges. The text frames it as a moment of realization, not a scolding or a demand for immediate cleanup.
---
Q3: What did the narrator associate with the finding of his growing-up moment?
Correct: Writing in his journal about no longer being "little."
Distractors: The act of fixing the old house. / Helping at the dairy for the first time. / The creation of a new friendship.
Explanation: The narrator explicitly links the finding to journaling his realization: "Today, I stopped being little." The other options are unrelated to his epiphany.
---
Q4: How did the farm’s environment contribute to his realization?
Correct: It forced him to confront responsibilities he hadn’t faced before.
Distractors: It made him homesick for the city. / It reminded him of his grandfather’s settler past. / It distracted him from his studies.
Explanation: The text describes the farm as a place where he had to "handle mistakes" and "fix things," unlike his childhood. Homesickness, nostalgia, or academic distraction aren’t mentioned.
---
Q5: What does the thesis of growing up refer to in this context?
Correct: The idea that childhood ends when responsibilities begin.
Distractors: The belief that growing up means leaving the farm. / The argument that children should never cry. / The theory that mistakes should always be fixed immediately.
Explanation: The narrator defines the thesis as "no longer a child, but someone expected to handle mistakes." The other options misrepresent the text’s focus on responsibility, not location, emotions, or urgency.



Topic: a personal essay on the biggest misconception people have about me

Text:
The biggest misconception people have about me is that I’m some kind of introverted loner who hides in caves to write. In reality, I’m the opposite—I thrive in unity with others. My friends often joke that I’m hilarious at parties, but the truth is, I’m just naturally outgoing. I love organizing events, leading discussions, and even hosting game nights where we all contribute ideas. People assume I’m quiet because I prefer deep conversations over small talk, but that doesn’t mean I avoid socializing.

Another misunderstanding comes from my organic lifestyle. Many think I’m judgmental about others’ choices, but I’m not. I simply believe in making informed decisions—whether it’s about food, finances, or even taking out a loan. I’ll happily explain my reasoning, but I don’t expect everyone to follow it. What surprises people most is my overall flexibility. I’m not rigid about rules; I adapt sufficiently to situations without losing my sense of humor.

I’ve even had to give an apology for this misconception after someone assumed I was cold because I didn’t send a birthday card. The reality? I was planning a surprise party and forgot to mention it. Leadership isn’t about perfection—it’s about communication, and I’ve learned that the best way to clear up misunderstandings is to laugh them off. After all, people remember the stories, not the stereotypes.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author say is the biggest misconception about their personality?
Correct: That they are an introverted loner who avoids social interactions.
Distractors: That they are overly critical of others’ lifestyles. / That they struggle with public speaking. / That they prefer working alone in isolation.
Explanation: The author explicitly states, “The biggest misconception people have about me is that I’m some kind of introverted loner who hides in caves to write.” The other options are incorrect because the text emphasizes their outgoing nature (“I thrive in unity with others”), lack of judgment (“I’m not judgmental about others’ choices”), and enjoyment of social events (“I love organizing events”).
---
Q2: Why does the author mention their organic lifestyle in the essay?
Correct: To clarify that their choices are personal, not judgmental.
Distractors: To argue that others should adopt the same lifestyle. / To explain why they avoid financial loans. / To describe their work environment.
Explanation: The author writes, “I simply believe in making informed decisions… but I don’t expect everyone to follow it.” This shows their lifestyle is personal, not prescriptive. The other options are incorrect because the text does not promote their lifestyle to others, link it to loans, or discuss work.
---
Q3: How does the author respond to the misunderstanding about their birthday card?
Correct: They admit they forgot but planned a surprise instead.
Distractors: They refused to apologize. / They blamed their friends for the mistake. / They claimed they were too busy with leadership tasks.
Explanation: The author says, “I’ve even had to give an apology… The reality? I was planning a surprise party and forgot to mention it.” The other options contradict the text, which shows accountability and humor (“laugh them off”).
---
Q4: What does the author imply about leadership in the essay?
Correct: It involves communication and adaptability, not perfection.
Distractors: It requires strict adherence to rules. / It is only effective in isolated settings. / It demands constant public speaking.
Explanation: The author states, “Leadership isn’t about perfection—it’s about communication,” and describes adapting “sufficiently” to situations. The other options are incorrect because the text rejects rigidity (“not rigid about rules”), isolation (“thrive in unity”), and overemphasis on speaking (“prefer deep conversations”).
---
Q5: Which word from the word list best describes the author’s attitude toward misunderstandings?
Correct: Hilarious
Distractors: Loan / Cave / Apology
Explanation: The author uses “hilarious” to describe how they and their friends react to misconceptions (“people remember the stories, not the stereotypes”). The other words (loan, cave, apology) are contextually unrelated to their attitude—loan refers to finances, cave to the misconception itself, and apology is an action, not an attitude.



Topic: a story about a time I had to navigate a complex bureaucratic system

Text:
Last summer, I moved to an exotic coastal suburb of Sydney, hoping for a fresh start. But my first challenge wasn’t the ocean breeze—it was the latest housing policy changes, which required me to declare my income within days of signing the lease. The process was confusing, and the local council’s website offered no clear guidance. Worse, the clerk at the office seemed to have negative assumptions about newcomers, especially those with foreign-sounding names. I felt like I was facing racism disguised as bureaucracy.

I spent weeks gathering documents, only to be told I was missing something trivial. Finally, after a heated argument (and a show of sympathy from a younger employee who admitted the system was broken), I got my resolution. The clerk even apologized, saying, “Apparently, we’re supposed to help, not make people feel like they owe us favors.” The experience left me exhausted but determined—bureaucracy might be slow, but persistence pays off.

Questions:
Q1: What was the narrator’s first challenge after moving to the new suburb?
Correct: Navigating confusing housing policy changes.
Distractors: Finding a job in the local market. / Dealing with aggressive neighbors. / Adjusting to the exotic climate.
Explanation: The text states the narrator’s first challenge was the latest housing policy changes, not job hunting, neighbors, or climate. The other options are unrelated to the bureaucratic hurdle described.
---
Q2: How did the council clerk initially treat the narrator?
Correct: With apparent hostility and negative assumptions.
Distractors: With professional courtesy and efficiency. / By offering immediate help and sympathy. / By ignoring the narrator’s presence.
Explanation: The text describes the clerk’s negative attitude and racism-like behavior (“negative assumptions about newcomers”). The other options contradict the narrator’s experience of frustration and discrimination.
---
Q3: What finally helped the narrator resolve the issue?
Correct: A younger employee’s sympathy and admission that the system was flawed.
Distractors: A formal complaint to a higher authority. / Threatening legal action. / Bribing the clerk with a gift.
Explanation: The sympathy of a younger employee and their acknowledgment of the system’s flaws (“the system was broken”) led to the resolution. The text mentions no legal threats, bribes, or formal complaints.
---
Q4: Why did the clerk apologize at the end?
Correct: Because the narrator proved persistence and exposed the system’s flaws.
Distractors: Because the narrator paid an extra fee. / Because the clerk realized the narrator was a government official. / Because the narrator threatened to report the clerk.
Explanation: The apology came after the narrator’s persistence and the clerk’s realization that the system was unfair (“we’re supposed to help, not make people feel like they owe us favors”). The other options are unsupported by the text.
---
Q5: What does the narrator imply about bureaucracy in general?
Correct: It is often slow and unfair, but persistence can lead to a resolution.
Distractors: It is always efficient if you follow the rules strictly. / It favors those who can afford bribes. / It is designed to punish outsiders.
Explanation: The narrator’s experience suggests bureaucracy is negative and flawed (“the system was broken”), but determination can overcome it. The other options overgeneralize or misrepresent the text’s tone (e.g., no mention of bribes or systemic punishment).



Topic: a personal story about a time I felt like an outsider and how I coped

Text:
When I was 16, my family moved from a small town to a bustling downtown area, and I immediately felt like an outsider. The local teens seemed to have their own unspoken rules, and I didn’t fit in. One day, a group of them accused me of being "too quiet" and "not one of them." The slight insult stung, but what hurt more was the way they treated me as if my accent and mannerisms were strange. I realized then that my differences—my moral values, my way of speaking—made me stand out in a way that felt uncomfortable.

I tried to alter my behavior to fit in, but it didn’t feel appropriate. Instead, I focused on finding my own community. I joined a debate club where people valued thoughtful discussions over conformity. There, I met others who didn’t care about racial or social stereotypes. Over time, I learned that being an outsider wasn’t a flaw—it just meant I had a different perspective. The experience taught me that true belonging comes from being yourself, not from trying to match someone else’s expectations.

Years later, I launched a podcast about cultural differences, partly inspired by that difficult time. I still remember the rub of rejection, but I also know how much stronger it made me.

Questions:
Q1: What was the main reason the narrator felt like an outsider in the new city?
Correct: Their accent and mannerisms made them stand out.
Distractors: They refused to follow the local fashion trends. / They were accused of stealing something. / They didn’t speak the local language fluently.
Explanation: The text states, "my accent and mannerisms were strange" and "they treated me as if my accent and mannerisms were strange," confirming the correct answer. The other options are not mentioned—fashion, theft, or language barriers are irrelevant to the narrator’s experience.
---
Q2: How did the narrator initially try to cope with feeling like an outsider?
Correct: They attempted to change their behavior to fit in.
Distractors: They moved back to their hometown. / They ignored the other teens completely. / They started a protest against the group.
Explanation: The text says, "I tried to alter my behavior to fit in," making this the correct choice. The other options contradict the text—there’s no mention of moving, ignoring others, or protesting.
---
Q3: What did the narrator realize about their differences after the experience?
Correct: They understood that being an outsider gave them a unique perspective.
Distractors: They regretted not speaking up sooner. / They decided to completely reject their old values. / They believed their differences were a weakness.
Explanation: The text explains, "I learned that being an outsider wasn’t a flaw—it just meant I had a different perspective," confirming the correct answer. The other options are unsupported—there’s no mention of regret, rejecting values, or seeing differences as a weakness.
---
Q4: Why was joining the debate club a turning point for the narrator?
Correct: It helped them find a community that valued individuality.

My grandfather had never spoken about her, and I wondered why she had been so important to him.

For weeks, I searched for answers, desperately flipping through old letters and diaries. The silence surrounding her only deepened my curiosity. One evening, while cleaning the attic, I found a yellowed newspaper clipping about a local victory in a horse race—dated the same year as the photograph. The winner’s name was the same as the woman’s. Gradually, the pieces began to fit: she must have been my great-grandmother, a champion jockey who had given up racing after a tragic accident. The locket, I realized, was her last connection to a life she had once loved but could no longer live.

As I held the locket, I felt less miserable about the mystery. Instead, I was filled with a quiet pride—my grandfather had passed down not just an object, but a story of resilience and hidden victory. I decided to find out more, tracing the woman’s racing career through old records. The more I learned, the more I understood why my grandfather had kept the locket all these years. It wasn’t just a keepsake; it was a reminder that even in silence, lives could hold extraordinary meaning.

One day, while researching in the local archives, I found a moving letter he wrote to his son about the wisdom he gained from surviving poverty. The letter mentioned a gang of young men who had protected him from a violent neighborhood aspect—a detail that made his past feel even more real.

Armed with this new information, I traveled to the city where he had lived. I equipped myself with old maps, a notebook, and a sense of determination. The journey wasn’t easy; the area had changed drastically, and some buildings had been demolished. Worse, I learned that the city had once been near a radiation testing site, and though officials claimed it was safe, I couldn’t shake the feeling that something was hurtful about the environment. Still, I pressed on, determined to uncover more.

One afternoon, I met an elderly woman who remembered my great-grandfather. She told me he had once won a scholarship to study engineering but had to drop out when his family needed money. Hearing this made me conscious of how much harder his life had been than I’d imagined. By the end of my trip, I had pieced together a clearer picture—not just of his struggles, but of the resilience that had shaped my own family.

Topic: a story about a time I had to live with a difficult person and what it taught me

Text:

She’d demand I prepare gourmet meals at midnight or adjust the thermostat to "exactly 18°C" while wearing a thick sweater. At first, I tried to meet her demands, but it quickly became clear that adequate care meant more than following orders—it meant understanding her needs.

Eleanor was humorous in a dry, sarcastic way, which made her company bearable. She’d joke about her "puzzle" of a brain, claiming she forgot things on purpose to keep life interesting. Yet, she opposed any attempts to simplify her routine, insisting on trading stories from her youth instead of watching TV. One evening, after I speculated aloud about her past, she confessed that her pure stubbornness had once cost her a job—but she’d never regretted it.

Living with her taught me patience. I learned that people, like puzzles, reveal their true shapes over time. Eleanor’s quirks weren’t flaws; they were layers of a life well-lived. By the end, I adjusted my approach: I stopped trying to change her and started listening. The experience wasn’t easy, but it was invaluable.

Topic: a fantasy story about a librarian who discovers the books can rewrite themselves

Over the following weeks, more books began to change. A biography of a 19th-century scientist acquired a chapter about time travel, while a children’s fairy tale inserted itself into a detective novel. Elara, though shaken, remained steady in her resolve. Her colleague, Marcus, refused to believe her at first, but after witnessing a dictionary electing to include a new word—"librariomancy"—he finally accompanied her in her investigation.

The library’s patrons, however, had their own interpretation of events. Some whispered about curses, others about government experiments. Elara, though, suspected something far stranger: the books were not just changing—they were acquiring memories, experiences, and even emotions from the readers who handled them. The more a book was read, the more it evolved, as if absorbing the collective imagination of its audience. Now, Elara faced a dilemma: should she reveal the truth to the world, or keep the library’s magical secret buried beneath the shelves?

Nevertheless, he pressed on, determined to register the new coastal inlets before the tides erased them.

By the third week, his crew began to grumble. "The land moves faster than we can chart it!" muttered the first mate. Voss only smiled. "Then we move faster." His obsession bordered on madness, but Zorvathia’s ever-changing geography demanded it. As he inked the final coordinates, the ground beneath him trembled. The continent was reshaping itself again.

Topic: a fantasy story where the main character realises that their world is an illusion

Text:
Lena had always lived in the quiet village of Eldermere, where life followed a predictable routine. She woke at dawn, worked in her father’s shop, and returned home by dusk. The villagers spoke of the world beyond the misty hills, but no one had ever left—or returned. One evening, as Lena folded laundry, she noticed something strange: the shadows moved when she wasn’t looking. At first, she thought it was her imagination, but then she saw her reflection in the mirror fold slightly out of sync with her own movements.

Curious, she followed the shadows into the forest, where she found a hidden door behind a waterfall. Inside, a random selection of objects lay scattered—books with no titles, a clock that ran backward, and a ticket to a lottery she didn’t remember entering. A voice whispered, “You’ve always had the agency to choose, but you never did.” Lena’s hands trembled as she realized her entire life might be an illusion, designed to keep her docile. The villagers’ routine wasn’t theirs to break; it was a script. The thought was unacceptable—she had spent her life believing she had no control.

She reached for the lottery ticket, and the world folded around her. Suddenly, she stood in a bustling city, where people moved freely, their faces filled with purpose. A man nearby dropped his coffee, and as Lena bent to help, she saw her own hand—older, but unmistakably hers. “You’ve been asleep,” he said. “Now you’re forward to waking up.” Lena gasped. The illusion had dominated her mind for years, but now, for the first time, she was free. The question that arose was simple: what would she do with her newfound reality?

While her reflection did move oddly, the initial suspicion came from the shadows. The lottery ticket was found after she entered the forest, not before.

Distractors: She was forced to follow the villagers’ rules against her will. / She secretly rebelled but was punished for it. / She chose to ignore the truth about her world.

Topic: a suspenseful story about a portal to a dimension where thoughts become reality

Text:
The old library in Blackwood Hollow was known for its exotic collection of forgotten books, but no one suspected the hidden door behind the comparative dusty shelves. Elias, a skeptical graduate student, had come to research obscure manuscripts—until he found the portal. It hummed faintly, its edges shimmering like liquid silver. Before he could question his unfortunate curiosity, he stepped through.

The other side was a world where thoughts shaped reality. A single glance at a crumbling wall made it vanish, replaced by a forest of towering trees. Elias tried to focus, but his mind raced: "I need to leave." Instantly, the portal reappeared, its glow pulsing like a warning. He stumbled back, his elbow scraping against the frame. Behind him, a gnarled worm of smoke slithered from the doorway, as if the dimension was accusing him of hesitation.

A voice echoed: "You must complete the contest before the veil closes." Elias had no time to decipher the rules. The air grew thick, and shadows preceded him, whispering fragments of forgotten languages. He clenched his fists—risky, he knew, but he had to escape. As he sprinted, the world twisted: his fear manifested as a storm of jagged teeth, his doubt as a chasm yawning beneath his feet. One wrong thought, and he’d be trapped forever.

Topic: an essay on the societal impact of the four-day work week

While some argue it reduces productivity, others claim it improves employees’ nutrition, mental health, and work-life balance. The results were neutral—no drop in efficiency, but a noticeable rise in job satisfaction. Critics, however, question whether such a model is relevant to all industries, especially those reliant on trading or continuous operations.

Supporters argue that shorter weeks allow employees to fulfil personal goals, such as spending time with family or pursuing hobbies. A signature achievement of the trial was the reduction in stress-related absences. Yet, skeptics beg to differ, pointing to potential challenges like overtime demands or the difficulty of maintaining a neutral stance on productivity. The subject remains debated, but one thing is clear: flexibility in work hours could reshape modern employment forever.

Over time, the numbers above people’s heads became a normal sight, though some still found the concept unsettling. A sincere effort was made to ensure the system didn’t discriminate, but debates continued about whether it truly benefited the entire population or just those who could afford better health.

Despite the controversy, Lumina’s experiment had one undeniable effect: people started paying more attention to their well-being. Even those who opposed the system often accompany their friends to gyms or health check-ups, hoping to improve their own numbers. The project remained a defining feature of Lumina, a promising but flawed attempt to reshape society through visibility.

Topic: a reflective essay on the concept of home in an increasingly globalized and mobile society

Text:
The idea of home has never felt more complicated than in today’s globalized world. For many, it is no longer a fixed address but a shifting concept tied to memory, belonging, and even economic survival. My own understanding of home evolved considerably when I moved from a small coastal village to a bustling city during a recession. The move was not by choice—my family, like many others, had to relocate to find work—but it forced me to question what truly defines a home.

In my village, home was a place of golden routines: the scent of saltwater in the air, the same path to school, and the unspoken bond between neighbors who had lived there for generations. The sea itself was a marker of our identity, a constant presence that shaped our daily lives. Yet, when we left, that stability vanished. The city offered opportunities, but it also stripped away the familiar. At first, I missed the simplicity of village life, the way my grandmother’s cheek would blush when she laughed, the way the settler community gathered for festivals that felt like a lifeline.

Over time, however, I realized home was not just a place but a feeling. It was the moments of connection—whether with my parents, old friends, or even strangers who shared my struggles. The recession had made us all more transient, but it also revealed how deeply home is tied to relationships. A house can be rented or sold, but the bond between people endures. Thus, in a world where mobility is the norm, perhaps the most apparent truth is that home is wherever we feel understood, wherever we are allowed to be ourselves.

Topic: an article exploring the ethics of digital legacies and social media profiles after death

Text:
In the digital age, the question of what happens to our online presence after death has become increasingly urgent. Many people broadly assume that their social media profiles will simply vanish, but the reality is far more complex. When someone passes away, their mate—whether a close friend or family member—often faces difficult decisions about preserving or deleting accounts. Some choose to memorialize profiles, turning them into digital shrines, while others prefer to erase all traces of the person’s online life.

The ethics of digital legacies are not straightforward. On one hand, keeping a profile active can provide comfort to grieving friends, offering a space to share memories. On the other, it raises concerns about privacy and the emission of outdated or even harmful content. For example, a person’s last post might contain acid remarks or private messages that could resurface years later, causing unintended pain. Platforms like Facebook allow users to set up legacy contacts, but these options are often overlooked until it’s too late.

A wise approach might involve creating a plan reasonably in advance. This could include designating someone to manage accounts, deciding which memories to preserve, and ensuring that sensitive data is securely deleted. Some argue that the best way to overcome the ethical dilemmas is to limit one’s digital footprint before death. However, with social media use exceeding 4 billion active users worldwide, this seems impractical for most.

Ultimately, the conversation around digital legacies forces us to confront deeper questions: What do we possess online, and who has the right to control it after we’re gone? The answers are not always clear, but one thing is certain—ignoring the issue will not make it disappear.

Questions:
Q1: What is the main ethical dilemma mentioned regarding digital legacies?
Correct: Whether to preserve or delete social media profiles after death.
Distractors: How to prevent hackers from accessing accounts. / Whether platforms should charge for memorializing profiles. / How to ensure all online content is legally protected.
Explanation: The text discusses the conflict between keeping profiles active for comfort and deleting them to avoid privacy risks. Hacking, charging for memorialization, and legal protection are not mentioned.
---
Q2: What does the text suggest about the emission of harmful content?
Correct: It could resurface years later and cause unintended pain.
Distractors: It is automatically filtered by social media algorithms. / It only affects the original poster’s family. / It is a rare occurrence in digital legacies.
Explanation: The text warns that outdated posts (e.g., acid remarks) may reappear and harm others. Algorithms, limited impact, or rarity are not discussed.
---
Q3: Why might designating a legacy contact be considered wise?
Correct: It allows someone to manage accounts according to the user’s wishes.
Distractors: It guarantees that all posts will be permanently deleted. / It prevents friends from accessing the profile. / It ensures the account will go viral after death.
Explanation: The text states that legacy contacts help manage accounts post-death. Permanent deletion, blocking friends, or virality are not mentioned.
---
Q4: How does the text describe the scale of social media use?
Correct: It exceeds 4 billion active users worldwide.
Distractors: It is declining due to privacy concerns. / It is limited to a few wealthy countries. / It is mostly used by young people.
Explanation: The text explicitly states the user count exceeds 4 billion. Decline, wealth limitations, or age restrictions are not supported.
---
Q5: What does the text imply about the best way to handle digital legacies?
Correct: Planning reasonably in advance is the most practical solution.
Distractors: Completely avoiding social media is the only ethical choice. / Letting platforms decide automatically is the safest option. / Waiting until death to make decisions is ideal.
Explanation: The text suggests proactive planning (e.g., designating a mate to manage accounts) as the best approach. Avoiding social media entirely, relying on platforms, or delaying decisions are dismissed as impractical or unwise.



Topic: a reflective essay on the value of solitude in a hyper-connected world

Text:
In a world where notifications buzz constantly and social media demands our attention, the pursuit of solitude feels like a rebellious act. Yet, as an analyst of human behavior, I’ve observed that even the most hyper-connected individuals crave moments of silence. Solitude isn’t about isolation—it’s about reclaiming space to think, create, and simply withdraw from the noise. Studies show that prolonged exposure to digital stimuli reduces creativity and deep thinking, turning us into fragmented creatures of habit rather than individuals capable of original output.

Take, for example, the operator of a 24/7 news network. Despite the coverage of global events, they admit to stealing 30 minutes daily to walk alone, away from screens. "It’s the only time I can process information without the pressure of immediate responses," they say. Convenience has become a trap—we sacrifice depth for efficiency, but solitude forces us to slow down. Even in a firm culture obsessed with productivity, employees who take solo breaks report higher focus and innovation. The irony? The more we connect, the lonelier we become. Solitude, then, isn’t a luxury—it’s a necessity for a mind unburdened by the constant chatter of the digital age.

Topic: an article about the paradox of choice - why more options often make us less happy

Text:
In a world where consumers are bombarded with endless choices—from streaming platforms offering thousands of shows to supermarkets stocked with hundreds of cereal brands—scientists have uncovered a surprising truth: more options often lead to less happiness. This phenomenon, known as the paradox of choice, suggests that when people face too many decisions, they become overwhelmed, anxious, and even resist making a choice at all.

Psychologists Barry Schwartz and Sheena Iyengar conducted experiments showing that participants who had to choose from a volume of jam flavors were less satisfied with their decision than those given fewer options. Similarly, job applicants overwhelmed by career paths reported higher stress levels. Even in rural areas, where fewer choices exist, people often feel less regret about their decisions.

The pressure to optimize every aspect of life—from diets to circuit training routines—adds to the burden. Social media amplifies this with curated lives, making people question their own choices. For example, a small business might struggle to stand out in a crowded market, even if its logo is unique, because consumers can’t chop through the noise to focus on what truly matters.

Interestingly, countries with high immigration rates, where cultural and lifestyle choices multiply, often see rising rates of decision paralysis. People may delay major life affairs, like marriage or career moves, because the fear of "wrong" choices paralyzes them. The solution? Limiting options to a manageable number—or learning to accept that some decisions don’t need perfection.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the relationship between choice and happiness?
Correct: More choices can reduce satisfaction because they increase anxiety.
Distractors: Fewer choices always lead to better decision-making. / People in rural areas are happier because they have no choices. / The paradox of choice only affects people with high incomes.
Explanation:
The text states that "more options often lead to less happiness" due to "overwhelm, anxiety, and resistance." The correct answer aligns with this idea. The distractors are incorrect because:
- "Fewer choices always lead to better decision-making" is too absolute (the text mentions manageable options, not necessarily fewer).
- "People in rural areas are happier because they have no choices" misrepresents the text (rural areas have fewer choices, which reduces regret, but not necessarily no choices).
- "The paradox only affects people with high incomes" is unsupported (the text discusses general consumer behavior, not income levels).
---
Q2: What example does the text use to illustrate the paradox of choice?
Correct: Participants choosing from a large volume of jam flavors were less satisfied.
Distractors: Job applicants with too many career options reported lower stress. / People in rural areas regret their decisions more. / Businesses with unique logos struggle because of immigration trends.
Explanation:
The text explicitly mentions "participants who had to choose from a volume of jam flavors were less satisfied." The distractors are incorrect because:
- "Job applicants with too many career options reported lower stress" contradicts the text (they reported higher stress).
- "People in rural areas regret their decisions more" is the opposite of what the text says (they feel less regret).
- "Businesses with unique logos struggle because of immigration trends" misinterprets the text (the issue is consumer overload, not immigration).
---
Q3: How does social media contribute to the paradox of choice?
Correct: It amplifies curated lives, making people question their own choices.
Distractors: It provides too many options for career paths. / It reduces the volume of information available. / It encourages people to make impulsive decisions.
Explanation:
The text explains that social media "amplifies this [paradox] with curated lives, making people question their own choices." The distractors are wrong because:
- "Too many options for career paths" is mentioned but not linked to social media.
- "Reduces the volume of information" contradicts the text (social media increases information).
- "Encourages impulsive decisions" is not supported (the text focuses on overthinking, not impulsivity).
---
Q4: Why might people delay major life decisions, according to the text?
Correct: The fear of making a "wrong" choice paralyzes them.
Distractors: They lack access to enough options. / They are influenced by immigration policies. / They prefer rural lifestyles over urban ones.
Explanation:
The text states: "People may delay major life affairs... because the fear of 'wrong' choices paralyzes them." The distractors are incorrect because:
- "Lack of options" is the opposite of the paradox (too many options cause delay).
- "Influenced by immigration policies" is unrelated to the text’s focus.
- "Prefer rural lifestyles" is not mentioned as a reason for delay.
---
Q5: What does the text suggest as a solution to decision paralysis?
Correct: Limiting options to a manageable number or accepting imperfect choices.
Distractors: Ignoring social media entirely. / Moving to rural areas to reduce choices. / Relying on unique logos to stand out.
Explanation:

- "Ignoring social media entirely" is extreme and not suggested.
- "Moving to rural areas" is not presented as a solution (it’s a context where fewer choices exist naturally).
- "Relying on unique logos" is irrelevant to personal decision-making.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of remote work

Text:
The origin of remote work can be traced back to the 1970s, but its rise in popularity exploded during the COVID-19 pandemic. Companies realized that employees could be just as productive outside the office, leading to a shift in workplace culture. One of the biggest advantages is flexibility—workers can set their own schedules, balance personal life, and avoid long commutes. This freedom often motivates them to perform better, as they feel trusted and valued. Additionally, remote work opens doors for businesses to hire talent globally, regardless of location, which can improve delivery times and innovation.

However, challenges exist. Without face-to-face interaction, teamwork can suffer, and miscommunication may arise, especially when relying on digital cues like emails or video calls. Some roles, such as those requiring hands-on training or specialization in niche fields, may struggle with remote setups. Another issue is the risk of burnout—blurring the account between work and home life can lead to overworking. Poor internet coverage or technical issues can also ruin productivity. Finally, not all industries thrive remotely; creative fields, like songwriting, may miss the inspiration of collaborating in person, where lyrics and ideas flow more naturally in shared spaces.

Ultimately, remote work is not a one-size-fits-all solution. While it offers unmatched convenience, companies must adapt policies to ensure fairness, engagement, and mental well-being. The key lies in finding a balance—perhaps a hybrid model where employees split time between home and office, maintaining both efficiency and human connection.

Distractors: Employees demanded better work-life balance. / Governments passed laws requiring remote options. / Technology finally became advanced enough to support it.
Explanation:
The text states, "its rise in popularity exploded during the COVID-19 pandemic," directly linking the pandemic to remote work’s growth. The other options are plausible but unsupported—work-life balance is mentioned as a benefit, not the cause; no laws are referenced; and while technology improved, the text doesn’t claim it was the main reason.

The text warns that "without face-to-face interaction... miscommunication may arise, especially when relying on digital cues like emails or video calls." The other options contradict the text: teamwork isn’t guaranteed to improve, meetings may still be needed, and time zones aren’t mentioned as a requirement.

Topic: a case study on the use of gamification in advertising

Text:
In 2021, a small tech startup called PlayAd launched a campaign to promote its new app using gamification—a strategy that turned advertising into an interactive game. The company faced a major challenge: low brand awareness and limited funding. Traditional ads were failing to grab attention, so PlayAd decided to create a viral challenge called "Ad Starve." Players had to complete daily tasks, such as watching ads, sharing them on social media, or completing quizzes, to earn points. The more they engaged, the closer they got to "defeating" a virtual monster that symbolized harmful advertising habits. The incentive? Players who reached the highest score won discounts on the app and were featured in a leaderboard.

The campaign was a victory. Within a month, engagement rates enhanced by 300%, and the app’s downloads doubled. Players loved the feedback loop—immediate rewards for participation kept them hooked. However, the real breakthrough came when PlayAd partnered with influencers to host live "ad bombing" sessions, where they collectively smashed the monster by watching ads together. This created a sense of community and urgency, making the campaign go viral.

Critics argued that gamification could be harmful if overused, turning ads into a distraction rather than a saving grace. But PlayAd’s data showed otherwise: users spent more time with the brand, and the app’s retention rate improved by 40%. The key was balancing fun with purpose—players weren’t just clicking mindlessly; they were learning about the product while enjoying the game.

Topic: an article about trends in urban planning

Text:
Urban planners are increasingly turning to innovative strategies to address the challenges of rapid population growth. One of the most promising trends is the concept of "15-minute cities", where residents can access essential services—like schools, shops, and parks—within a short walk or bike ride. This idea, first proposed by French urbanist Carlos Moreno in 2016, has gained traction globally, with cities like Paris and Melbourne establishing pilot projects. Critics argue that such plans require a genuine commitment from local governments, not just symbolic gestures.

Another key development is the rise of "smart cities", which integrate technology to improve efficiency. For example, Barcelona’s "Superblocks" initiative uses sensors to monitor air quality and traffic, allowing authorities to adjust policies in real time. While some see this as a rocket toward progress, others worry about the high costs and digital divide. A recent bill in the U.S. Senate proposed funding for smart infrastructure, but opponents claim it lacks a clear package of safeguards for privacy.

Public opinion remains divided. A survey revealed that 68% of respondents were impressed by green urban designs, yet many doubt whether cities can deliver on their promises. The hypothesis that compact cities reduce car dependency has been tested in studies, but results vary. For instance, Copenhagen’s bike-friendly policies have cut emissions, while London’s congestion charge failed to adjust to inflation for years, losing its signature effectiveness.

Lena and Mark had never met in person, but their friendship was one of the most ongoing and meaningful connections in both of their lives. They bonded over a shared scholarship in digital art, exchanging ideas in a virtual forum where they spent hours discussing projects, techniques, and even life goals. Their conversations were so deep that Lena often joked they knew each other’s judgement better than their own families did.

One day, Mark posted a gorgeous digital painting he had created—a surreal landscape that blended technology and nature. Lena was stunned. “This is entirely different from your usual style,” she wrote. “You should exhibit it somewhere.” Mark hesitated. “I’ve never shown my work outside the forum. What if people don’t like it?” Lena replied, “Then they’re missing out. But if you target the right gallery, you’ll get maximum exposure.”

Their debate sparked an idea: why not meet in person to discuss it further? The thought terrified Mark—what if the friendship faded once they stepped out of the virtual world? But Lena insisted, “Real connections aren’t just about pixels. They’re about shared energy, laughter, and maybe even a bad coffee shop.” Thus, they planned a meeting in a city halfway between them.

The day arrived, and Mark arrived early, clutching his tablet with the painting. Lena was already there, holding two overpriced lattes. “You made it,” she said, grinning. Mark exhaled. “I did. And… I’m glad.” As they walked to the gallery, he realized something: their friendship had always been special, but now it felt entirely new—richer, warmer, and undeniably real.

Topic: a suspenseful story about an elevator in a skyscraper that occasionally stops at a floor not in the building's plans

Text:
The lively skyscraper on Fifth Avenue was known for its modern construction and sleek design, but its elevators had a strange reputation. Employees whispered about the "ghost floor"—a level that didn’t exist on any register or blueprint. The elevator would occasionally stop between floors, its doors sliding open to reveal a dimly lit corridor with no representative signs or emergency exits.

One evening, security guard Marcus decided to investigate. He pressed the button for the 47th floor, but the elevator advanced to the 47.5 mark. The doors opened to reveal a narrow hallway lined with old wooden panels, smelling faintly of damp and something metallic. A single lightbulb flickered above a rusted mail slot. Inside, Marcus found a small package wrapped in yellowed paper, stamped with a date from 1989. His hands trembled as he unwrapped it—inside was a key labeled "Property of V. Krane."

The next day, the building’s manager dismissed the incident as a malfunction, but Marcus couldn’t shake the feeling that something was wrong. He researched V. Krane and discovered the man had been a representative for an affordable housing project that had mysteriously been abandoned mid-construction. The key, Marcus realized, might lead to a hidden floor where the project’s secrets—and perhaps its spice of danger—still lingered.

That night, he returned to the elevator. This time, it stopped at floor 47.7. The corridor stretched longer, the air thicker. At the end, a door stood slightly ajar, revealing a room furnished with 1980s office chairs and a typewriter. A single sheet of paper lay on the desk, dated the day the project was supposed to open. It read: "Do not withdraw your support. The ideal is worth the risk."

Marcus’s breath caught. The ideal of affordable housing—or something far darker? Before he could decide, the elevator lurched, and the doors slammed shut. The next morning, the ghost floor vanished from the register again, as if it had never existed. But Marcus kept the key in his pocket, wondering what other floors the building might hide—and what they might demand in return.

Her colleagues warned her to abandon the research, fearing ridicule. But Elara couldn’t ignore the object’s pull. Hence, she secretly studied it in her lab, convinced it held the key to unlocking a lost technology. The night she finally deciphered the symbols, the object pulsed once—then vanished, leaving behind only a faint scent of ozone. Some called it a trick of the light; Elara called it proof that the impossible was real.

Topic: a review of different architectural approaches to building in earthquake-prone zones

Text:
Earthquake-prone regions demand innovative architectural solutions, and engineers worldwide have developed strategies to address this challenge. One approach, devoted to flexibility, involves designing buildings that can sway with seismic waves rather than resist them. This method, often cited in studies by the International Institute of Seismology, allows structures to possess a degree of movement, reducing damage. Critics argue, however, that such designs may not be appropriate for densely populated urban areas where space is limited.

Another school of thought emphasizes documenting traditional techniques that have withstood earthquakes for centuries. In Japan, for example, wooden frameworks with flexible joints have been defended by historians as effective against tremors. Yet, modern diversity in construction materials complicates efforts to revive these methods. Some architects propose hybrid systems, combining steel reinforcements with adaptive foundations, but these require extensive enquiry into local geology and wind load factors.

While no single solution dominates, the institute’s latest report highlights the need for tailored approaches. Whether prioritizing flexibility, tradition, or innovation, the key lies in balancing engineering precision with cultural context. The debate continues, but one truth remains: earthquake-resistant design must evolve as quickly as the disasters themselves.

Topic: an article about the architecture of modern libraries

Text:
Modern libraries have evolved far beyond their traditional image as dusty repositories of books. Today, they are dynamic spaces designed to inspire creativity, foster learning, and even generate revenue through community events and partnerships. Architects now prioritize functionality, sustainability, and adaptability, ensuring these institutions remain relevant in the digital age.

One striking trend is the integration of super-efficient energy systems. Libraries like the origin-alistic Oslo Public Library in Norway, with its sloping green roof, demonstrate how design can reduce energy costs while creating a welcoming atmosphere. Such innovations help maintain long-term viability, as rising operational expenses threaten smaller institutions.

However, not all changes are purely practical. Some libraries experiment with bold aesthetics, like the junior-friendly "Library of Things" in Copenhagen, which offers tools for borrowing—from 3D printers to musical instruments. Critics argue that such designs may distract from the core mission, but proponents claim they attract new users who might otherwise avoid libraries entirely.

The shift toward interactive spaces also reflects a broader cultural fever for experiential learning. While some worry this trend dilutes the library’s scholarly origin, others argue it’s a natural evolution. For example, the Vancouver Public Library’s "Living Lab" lets visitors test prototypes, blurring the line between library and innovation hub.

Yet, challenges remain. Balancing cutting-edge design with accessibility requires careful planning. A surgeon-like precision is needed to ensure every detail—from seating arrangements to digital interfaces—serves the community appropriately. Short-term funding fluctuations further complicate efforts to sustain these transformations.

Ultimately, the future of library architecture lies in striking a balance: honoring tradition while embracing innovation, ensuring these spaces remain both timeless and timely.
