Topic: a story about the experience of returning to work after a long-term absence and feeling like a stranger

Text:
When I returned to work after a two-year leave, I felt like an outsider in my own office. My status as a former employee had faded, and my colleagues seemed to have moved on without me. The first day, I sat in a meeting where someone mentioned a new manufacturing project I had no knowledge of. My observation skills, once sharp, felt rusty—like trying to pilot an aircraft after years of ground training.

I had taken a break to care for a family member, but now, back at my desk, I struggled to keep up. My manager assigned me a portion of the old projects, assuming I’d catch up quickly. Instead, I failed to understand even elementary updates, like the new software everyone used. During an enquiry about my progress, I admitted I was lost. The team’s patience was kind, but I could tell they saw me as a dot on the organizational chart—someone who didn’t belong.

By week three, I realized I needed to prove my worth. I started attending every meeting, even if I didn’t contribute much. Slowly, I rebuilt my confidence, but the first month was a struggle. The office felt like a foreign place, and I wondered if I’d ever fit in again.

The map pointed to a hidden chamber beneath the ruins of the library’s eastern wing. With the demand for his research growing, Elias knew he could not delay.

At the chamber’s center stood a massive stone pedestal, and upon it rested a single, unopened scroll. Elias’s hands trembled as he unrolled it—inside were not just ancient texts, but evidence of a lost civilization’s independence, one that had mastered technologies far beyond their time. The scroll’s final words chilled him: "The knowledge here will change the world. Possess it wisely."

Even the schedule of these retreats—often demanding 12-hour days—can harm rather than heal, leaving spectators (and sometimes participants) questioning whether the focus is truly on well-being or profit. The industry’s ability to preserve its reputation depends on how adequately it addresses these concerns. Without stricter oversight, wellness risks becoming another trendy self-help scam, where the real cure is skepticism.

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of minimalism in interior design

Text:
Minimalism in interior design has become one of the most popular trends in recent years, but its rise hasn’t been without controversy. At its prime, minimalism focuses on simplicity, functionality, and the removal of unnecessary clutter. This approach can create a serene and organized workplace, reducing stress and improving productivity. For many, the clean lines and uncluttered spaces align perfectly with their perception of modern living—efficiency and calmness take center stage. However, critics argue that minimalism can sometimes feel sterile or confusing, especially when pushed too far. Without thoughtful decoration, a space may lack warmth or personality, making it feel cold and unwelcoming.

Another advantage of minimalism is its ability to encourage diversity in design choices. By stripping away excess, homeowners and designers are forced to focus on what truly matters, leading to more intentional and meaningful selections. This can also make feedback from users more straightforward, as there are fewer elements to critique. Yet, minimalism isn’t always the best fit for every lifestyle. For families or those with numerous hobbies, a clutter-free home might feel restrictive. Some argue that minimalism can even border on bombing the soul by eliminating the joy of surrounding oneself with cherished objects.

Establishing a minimalist space requires discipline, but the payoff can be significant. For those who thrive in environments that prioritize order and simplicity, minimalism offers a refreshing alternative to the overwhelming entertaining chaos of traditional decor. However, striking the right balance is key—too little detail can leave a space feeling empty, while too much can defeat the purpose entirely.

Topic: a commentary on the modern phenomenon of over-posting a travel experience

Text:
In the age of Instagram, travel has become less about discovery and more about performance. The phenomenon of over-posting—where every meal, every sunset, and even the act of unpacking luggage is shared online—has turned vacations into a critical competition for likes. Economists argue that this trend reflects a deeper societal shift: the need for constant validation in an urban world where real connections often feel scarce.

Consider the traveler who spends hours framing the perfect shot of a county cottage, only to post it with a caption like, "Living my best life." What’s missing? The soul of the experience—the unfiltered moments, the exhaustion after a long hike, or the awkward silence when the referee of a local festival mispronounces your name. Over-posting strips travel of its authenticity, turning it into a curated database of highlights designed to beat the algorithm, not the trail.

The irony? Many of these posts are acceptable by social standards but hollow in reality. A study found that 68% of travelers admit to exaggerating their adventures online. Whether it’s photoshopping a cloudy sky or claiming a "once-in-a-lifetime" experience that was merely pleasant, the pressure to outdo others has led to an eruption of performative wanderlust. The question remains: When does sharing become showing off, and when does it become stealing the joy of the journey itself?

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break

Text:
The moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break was during a psychology lecture at university. Our professor, a sophisticated woman with a sharp wit, discussed how trauma shapes behavior. She mentioned a concept called "intergenerational transmission," where parents unconsciously repeat harmful patterns from their own upbringing. I sat there, my pen hovering over my notebook, suddenly seeing myself in her words.

My father had been a terrorist in his youth, not in the violent sense, but in the way he dominated family life—impatient, demanding, and quick to anger. As a child, I had vowed never to be like him. Yet, in my early twenties, I found myself snapping at my partner over small things, my voice rising just like his used to. The worst part? I justified it with the same excuses he had: "They don’t understand me." That day in class, I understood the truth—I was replicating his troop-like control, not over a rebel group, but over my own emotions.

Subsequently, I sought therapy, but the real turning point came when my partner, exasperated, called me out: "You’re not the boss of me." The words stung, but they were necessary. I had to confront the fact that my cute childhood rebellion had curdled into something uglier. The chairman of my student council, a man I admired for his calm leadership, once told me, "Leaders don’t rule; they guide." I realized I had been trying to rule my relationships instead of guiding them with empathy.

Breaking the cycle wasn’t easy. It required admitting I was wrong, apologizing, and actively practicing patience—something my father had never done. But that lecture was the spark. Psychology gave me the language to name what I was doing, and I finally had the courage to change.

Q1: What initially triggered the narrator’s realization about repeating his father’s behavior?
Correct: A psychology lecture on intergenerational trauma.
Distractors: A conversation with his partner / A childhood memory / His father’s sudden apology.
Explanation: The text states the realization came "during a psychology lecture" where the professor discussed "intergenerational transmission." The partner’s confrontation happened after the realization, not before. Childhood memories and the father’s apology are never mentioned.
---
Q2: How does the narrator describe his father’s personality?
Correct: Impatient and domineering.
Distractors: Gentle and nurturing / Charismatic and humorous / Quiet and reserved.
Explanation: The narrator calls his father "impatient, demanding, and quick to anger," which aligns with domineering behavior. The other options contradict the text’s portrayal of his father as controlling and volatile.

Correct: A troop-like control.
Distractors: A silent shadow / A broken chain / A stormy sea.

Distractors: He directly criticized the narrator’s behavior / He offered therapy / He became the narrator’s mentor.

Distractors: It ended their relationship / It made his partner forgive him instantly / It was the first time he had apologized in his life.

Their argument had escalated after Lena publicly criticized Mark’s ethical stance on a compulsory hospital policy, calling it "cold and mechanical." Mark, offended, accused her of treating him like a creature from a textbook case rather than a human being.

Let’s talk."

Mark admitted he’d been defensive because the policy was personal—his father had died in a hospital where similar rules had failed him. Lena, realizing her words had unintentionally revived his pain, softened. "I didn’t mean to unconsciously hurt you," she murmured. They left the café with a handshake, not fully resolved but no longer at odds.

Topic: an essay on whether smartphones should be banned in schools

Text:
The debate over whether smartphones should be banned in schools has intensified in recent years. While some argue that these devices distract students and hinder learning, others believe they can be useful tools when used responsibly. However, the risks often outweigh the benefits.

One major concern is the illusion of productivity smartphones create. Students may believe they are multitasking by checking messages or browsing social media while pretending to study. In reality, this habit reduces focus and academic performance. Research shows that even the mere presence of a phone on a desk can lower concentration levels, making it harder for students to absorb information.

Another issue is the potential for cyberbullying and inappropriate content. Schools cannot always monitor what students transmit online, leaving them vulnerable to harmful messages or predators. Additionally, the temptation to use phones during exams or group projects can lead to cheating, undermining the integrity of education.

Some schools argue that smartphones could be useful in emergencies, such as contacting parents or inserting important updates. However, this benefit is often outweighed by the risks. For example, a student distracted by a phone might cause a crash if they are walking near traffic or fail to pay attention in class. In such circumstances, the device becomes a liability rather than an asset.

A few institutions have experimented with allowing phones under strict rules, but enforcement remains difficult. Even if students agree to turn off their devices, peer pressure and the fear of missing out (FOMO) make compliance nearly impossible. Some parents and educators suggest that schools should work with technology corporations to develop better digital literacy programs, but until then, a temporary ban may be the safest option.

Ultimately, the decision depends on the school’s priorities. While smartphones offer convenience, their presence in classrooms often creates more problems than solutions. Until clearer guidelines and safeguards are established, many educators believe a ban is the most effective way to protect students’ learning environments.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the "illusion of productivity" related to smartphones?
Correct: Students may think they are studying effectively while using their phones, but their focus is actually reduced.
Distractors: Smartphones help students feel more organized when used during study sessions. / The illusion refers to how teachers perceive students’ productivity. / Students believe their phones improve their memory and retention.
Explanation: The text states that smartphones create an "illusion of productivity" because students pretend to study while distracted by messages or social media, which actually reduces focus. The distractors are incorrect because the text does not mention organization, teacher perceptions, or memory improvement.
---
Q2: Why does the text mention the word "insert" in the context of smartphones?
Correct: To describe how smartphones can be used to share important updates or messages.
Distractors: To explain how students insert their phones into charging ports during class. / To refer to inserting educational apps into school curricula. / To discuss how phones are physically inserted into lockers.
Explanation: The text uses "inserting" to mean sending or sharing updates (e.g., emergency messages). The other options are unrelated—charging ports, educational apps, or lockers are not mentioned.
---

Topic: a story about a friendship that ended not with a fight but with a slow, mutual drifting apart

Daniel was consistent in his curiosity; he’d ask questions that made me think deeper, like how a single flame could both warm and burn.

For years, our friendship was effortless. We’d meet for coffee after shifts, talk for hours about books or travel, and even planned a road trip together. But life advanced in ways we couldn’t keep up with. Daniel got a job in another city, and while we talked every week, the calls grew shorter. One evening, after a long silence, he mentioned he’d started dating someone who lived nearby. It wasn’t a fight—just a quiet realization that we’d drifted apart, literally and emotionally. The last time we saw each other, we stood awkwardly at a train station, our elbows brushing as we hugged. No anger, no tears—just the weight of time passing.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was jealous of a friend's success

Text:
Last summer, I spent a month in a small coastal village where time seemed to move at a different pace. The universal rhythm of waves hitting the shore and the scent of salt in the air made everything feel simpler. Among the few locals, there was a young fisherman named Leo, whose life seemed effortlessly perfect. He had just won a regional competition, and his status in the village skyrocketed overnight. Everyone praised his skill, and even the mayor mentioned him in a speech.

At first, I admired his success. But one evening, as we sat by the fire, Leo casually mentioned he was saving up to buy a boat—something I’d dreamed of doing for years. That’s when it hit me: I wasn’t just impressed. I was jealous. The nerve to admit it made my face burn, but the truth was undeniable. While I’d spent years stuck in a scheme of temporary jobs, Leo had built something real. His participation in the community was genuine, while mine felt like an infection of half-hearted attempts.

The worst part? I couldn’t even blame him. His success wasn’t equivalent to my failures—it was just different. That night, I realized envy wasn’t about what others had; it was about what I’d let evil (my own fear of change) keep me from having.

Topic: a story about how an unusual compromise resolved a conflict

Text:
The small village of Meadowgrove had always been divided between the old and the young. The elders, who had lived there for generations, refused to accept the changes brought by technology. They dominated the village council, insisting on keeping traditions alive, while the younger generation, eager for progress, wanted to modernize. Tensions grew until one day, a compromise was reached—but not the one anyone expected.

The village’s wise old librarian, Mrs. Holloway, had spent years observing the conflict. She noticed how the young people’s frustration was making the elders even more stubborn, and how the elders’ resistance was reinforcing the young people’s anger. One evening, after a particularly depressing meeting where both sides accused each other of being unreasonable, Mrs. Holloway suggested an unusual idea: a village-wide storytelling competition. The catch? Half the stories had to be told using only traditional methods—oral storytelling, handwritten manuscripts, or even carved wood—and the other half could use any technological tools available.

At first, both sides suspected it was a trick. The elders feared the young would dominate the competition with flashy presentations, while the young worried the old would refuse to participate. But when the opening ceremony arrived, something unexpected happened. The elders, though reluctant, began sharing stories passed down for centuries, their voices rich with emotion. Meanwhile, the young used digital tools to create interactive tales, blending old myths with modern animations. As the competition progressed, the two groups realized they had more in common than they thought. The elders admired the creativity of the young, and the young respected the depth of the old stories. By the end, the village had not just resolved its conflict but also created something beautiful—proof that compromise, when truly wise, can turn division into unity.

The other options are incorrect because the competition was designed to be inclusive (not to prove superiority or force methods), and there’s no mention of making the elders admit fault.

Explanation: The text says the elders "feared the young would dominate the competition," showing their suspicion. The other options contradict the text—they were reluctant but not immediately enthusiastic, they did participate, and there’s no mention of it being depressing.

Correct: They blended old myths with modern animations.
Distractors: They ignored the traditional stories completely. / They used technology only to criticize the elders. / They focused solely on creating flashy presentations.
Explanation: The text specifies that the young "used digital tools to create interactive tales, blending old myths with modern animations," meaning they integrated tradition with technology. The other options are incorrect because they didn’t ignore tradition, criticize, or focus only on flashiness.

Distractors: It made the elders realize technology was unnecessary. / It forced the young to abandon their modern ideas. / It left the conflict unresolved but less intense.

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of developing tourism

Text:
Tourism has become one of the world’s largest industries, reshaping economies and cultures formerly dominated by agriculture or manufacturing. While its growth fuels local economies—creating jobs and boosting infrastructure—it also brings challenges. In coastal towns, for example, the desire for visitors has led to golden opportunities for hotels and restaurants, but it has also strained resources, making clean water and waste management less acceptable standards. Some destinations, like Venice, now face temporarily closed areas to protect historic sites from overcrowding.

Critics argue that mass tourism can erode local traditions. In Bali, for example, sacred ceremonies are sometimes performed openly for tourists, altering their spiritual significance. Meanwhile, the steam of cruise ships pollutes the air, and the heel of a tourist’s boot can damage ancient mosaics in places like Pompeii. Yet, tourism also improves global ratings for countries, attracting investment and raising living standards.

Balancing these effects requires careful planning. Some resorts now offer "slow tourism," encouraging visitors to explore tissue-thin layers of culture rather than rushing through landmarks. Others limit visitor numbers temporarily to preserve ecosystems. The key lies in sustainable practices—ensuring tourism benefits communities without sacrificing their identity or environment.

This means traditions are changed, not preserved or replaced entirely. The distractors misrepresent the text by suggesting tourism either ignores traditions or replaces them completely, which is not supported by the nuanced argument about alteration.

Topic: an essay about the benefits of playing computer games

Text:
In today’s fast-paced world, computer games are often seen as a waste of time, but research shows they offer distinct benefits beyond entertainment. One surprising advantage is their role in protection against stress. A weekly gaming session can act as a mental escape, allowing players to unwind after a long day. Studies reveal that gamers often feel more satisfied with their lives, thanks to the positive emotions games evoke—whether through adventure, problem-solving, or teamwork.

Games also improve cognitive skills. Strategy titles, for example, sharpen memory and critical thinking, while fast-paced action games enhance reflexes. Surprisingly, even casual mobile games can boost hand-eye coordination, useful in everyday tasks like typing or catching a falling object. Some gamers even use games to learn new info, such as history or languages, through interactive storytelling.

Environmentally, gaming can be eco-friendly. Many gamers now prefer digital copies over physical copies, reducing waste. Additionally, cloud gaming minimizes the need for powerful hardware, lowering energy use. For those who enjoy offline play, retro consoles or energy-efficient devices (like those used in greenhouse farming simulations) prove that gaming can align with sustainability.

Physical health isn’t left behind either. Motion-controlled games encourage movement—players might find themselves laughing as they dodge virtual obstacles, their heel taps or wind-milling arms keeping them active. Fitness trackers in games like Ring Fit Adventure turn exercise into a fun challenge, blending tech with wellness.

Finally, gaming fosters social connections. Online multiplayer games let friends collaborate or compete, strengthening bonds. Even solo play can reduce loneliness, as communities form around shared interests. Whether jotting down strategies in a notebook or streaming gameplay, gaming has become a modern way to connect.

Topic: a case study of a company making a positive impact on the environment and society

Text:

The founder of EcoBloom, a sustainable packaging company, was among the victims’ neighbors. Determined to turn loss into change, she launched EcoBloom via a crowdfunding campaign, using recycled ocean plastic to create biodegradable products. The company quickly gained attention for its stunning designs and commitment to reducing waste.

Today, EcoBloom employs members of the local tribe, offering training and fair wages. Their input has shaped products like reusable lunch boxes and plantable seed paper—items that decompose naturally. The company also partners with schools wherever possible, teaching children about sustainability. Critics initially opposed the idea, arguing that eco-friendly packaging was too expensive, but sales proved them wrong. Within five years, EcoBloom expanded globally, proving that profit and planet can coexist.

Topic: an essay on whether zoos should be banned

Text:
The debate over whether zoos should be banned is a complex subject that involves ethical, scientific, and psychological perspectives. While some argue that zoos provide a secure environment for endangered species, others claim that animals suffer from confinement, even if their basic needs—such as nutrition and shelter—are met. Critics also point to the psychological impact on animals, suggesting that captivity can lead to abnormal behaviors, such as pacing or self-harm, which resemble symptoms seen in humans under extreme stress.

Proponents of zoos, however, highlight their role in conservation and education. Many zoos employ skilled veterinarians and researchers who work to protect species from extinction. For example, overseas breeding programs have successfully reintroduced animals like the California condor into the wild. Additionally, zoos often motivate visitors to support wildlife protection, raising awareness about threats like habitat loss.

Yet, the argument becomes more complicated when considering cultural differences. In some countries, animals like elephants or tigers are kept in mosques or temples as symbols of spiritual significance, blurring the line between conservation and exploitation. Meanwhile, the idea of a "zoo" as a place of entertainment has evolved, with modern facilities emphasizing naturalistic habitats. Still, the question remains: Can we truly evaluate the welfare of animals in captivity without risking the perception of turning them into monsters of human curiosity?

Correct: can cause abnormal behaviors in animals due to psychological stress.
Distractors: always fail to provide adequate nutrition for animals. / only benefit wealthy countries with overseas breeding programs. / are primarily designed for entertainment rather than conservation.
Explanation:
The text states that "animals suffer from confinement... which can lead to abnormal behaviors, such as pacing or self-harm," directly linking captivity to psychological distress. The other options are incorrect because:
- Adequate nutrition is mentioned as a basic need that is met, not a failure.
- Overseas breeding programs are presented as a positive contribution, not a flaw.
- The text does not claim zoos are primarily for entertainment, though it acknowledges their role in education and conservation.
---

Correct: employing skilled professionals to protect endangered species.
Distractors: allowing visitors to interact directly with wild animals. / replacing natural habitats with artificial ones. / ignoring the cultural significance of animals in some societies.

The text explicitly mentions that zoos "employ skilled veterinarians and researchers who work to protect species from extinction." The other options are incorrect because:
- Direct interaction is not mentioned as a conservation method.

Correct: assessing whether animals in zoos are truly well-cared-for.
Distractors: calculating the financial costs of running a zoo. / determining the number of visitors each year. / deciding which animals should be bred in captivity.

The sentence reads, "Can we truly evaluate the welfare of animals in captivity?" This implies judging their well-being, not financial or visitor data. Breeding decisions are mentioned but not tied to the word evaluate in this context.

The text states that such practices "blur the line between conservation and exploitation," suggesting the debate is nuanced, not absolute. The other options are incorrect because:

Topic: an essay on how virtual reality might change education in the future

Text:
The idea that virtual reality (VR) will revolutionize education is no longer speculative—it is evident from the rapid growth of immersive technologies in classrooms worldwide. Traditional teaching methods, rooted in lectures and textbooks, are being challenged by VR’s ability to create interactive, three-dimensional environments. For instance, students can now explore ancient Rome as if they were there, dissect virtual frogs without ethical concerns, or even attend a chemistry lab where molecules stand before them in real time. Such experiences make abstract concepts tangible, bridging the gap between theory and practice.

Critics argue that VR could disrupt the convention of standardized examinations, replacing them with assessments that test problem-solving in simulated real-world scenarios. While this shift may seem radical, it aligns with the growing recognition that rote memorization is an exception, not the rule, in modern skill development. Proponents of VR education also highlight its potential to secure learning by reducing distractions—students immersed in a virtual museum, for example, are less likely to be tempted by digital theft of attention (e.g., multitasking with social media).

Beyond academics, VR could transform creative fields. Art students might collaborate on digital artwork in shared virtual studios, while history buffs could debate events as if they were witnesses to them. Even professional association programs, like medical training, could benefit from VR simulations that allow safe, repeated practice. The technology’s most beneficial impact, however, may lie in accessibility: VR can bring high-quality education to remote or underserved communities, democratizing opportunities once limited by geography or resources.

Yet challenges remain. High costs, technical barriers, and the need for teacher training could slow adoption. Still, as VR becomes more affordable and user-friendly, its role in education will likely expand, reshaping how—and what—we learn.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the future of standardized examinations in VR-enhanced education?
Correct: They may be replaced by assessments focused on real-world problem-solving.
Distractors: They will become more difficult to pass. / They will be entirely eliminated. / They will rely more on memorization.
Explanation:
The text states that VR could "disrupt the convention of standardized examinations, replacing them with assessments that test problem-solving in simulated real-world scenarios." This directly supports the correct answer. The distractors are incorrect because:
- "More difficult to pass" is not mentioned.
- "Entirely eliminated" is an overstatement—the text suggests a shift, not total removal.
- "Rely more on memorization" contradicts the emphasis on practical, interactive learning.

- Blocking the internet is not mentioned.

However, critics argue that without regulatory enforcement, these efforts remain symbolic. The sense of urgency many feel is entirely justified, but without corporate accountability, even the most committed individuals struggle to cope with the scale of the crisis.

History offers tales of resistance, such as the 1970s anti-nuclear protests, which observed temporary victories before corporate interests reasserted control. Today, while activists and consumers push for green policies, corporations—opposed to rapid change—often lobby against stricter regulations. The tension between personal responsibility and corporate accountability thus remains unresolved, leaving many to question whether incremental change is enough.

Topic: an essay on the psychology of risk-taking: why some people seek danger and others avoid it

Text:
The psychology of risk-taking reveals fascinating differences between individuals. Some people thrive on danger, while others avoid it at all costs. Researchers suggest that personality traits, upbringing, and even biology play a role. For instance, thrill-seekers often exhibit high levels of novelty-seeking, a trait linked to dopamine production in the brain. Their loyal following in extreme sports communities reinforces this behavior, as social recognition fuels their motivation.

On the opponent side, risk-averse individuals may have had experiences that discourage them from taking chances. Childhood trauma, a disability that limits physical ability, or witnessing accidents can shape cautious behavior. Even small setbacks, like a sprained wrist, might make someone more hesitant in the following years.

Interestingly, cultural angle also matters. In some societies, risk-taking is celebrated, while in others, safety is prioritized. Even the completion of a high-stakes task—like skydiving—can feel rewarding, but editing out fear requires mental strength. Ultimately, whether someone seeks danger or avoids it depends on a mix of genetics, environment, and personal history.

Topic: a speculative essay on what one piece of knowledge should be passed to future generations

Text:
If we could preserve only one piece of knowledge for future generations, what should it be? Many would argue for scientific breakthroughs—cures for diseases or renewable energy—but I propose something far simpler yet more profound: the art of storytelling. Not just the tale itself, but the skill to interpret its deeper meaning.

Consider the settlers of ancient civilizations. They built highways and cities, but their true legacy was the myths they passed down. These stories weren’t just entertainment; they encoded survival strategies, moral lessons, and warnings about the natural world. A well-told tale could teach a child to recognize poisonous plants or predict storms by reading the zone between mountains. Today, we underestimate this concrete wisdom. We rank facts by their immediate wealth of data, but forget that knowledge must be lived, not just memorized.

History shows that societies frequent collapse when they lose their stories. Without them, people become immune to the lessons of the past, repeating the same mistakes. A storyteller, however, bridges generations. They turn abstract ideas into vivid images—like a farmer explaining why a certain crop thrives in the floodplain, or a warrior describing the courage of an ancestor who stood alone. This isn’t nostalgia; it’s a survival tool.

Future generations won’t need more algorithms if they can’t interpret the world around them. They’ll need the ability to see patterns in chaos, to find meaning in silence, and to pass that meaning forward. So let’s teach them not just to read, but to listen—to the whispers of the wind, the cracks in the earth, and the voices of those who came before.

Topic: an essay discussing the ethics of using animals in medical research

Text:
The debate over animal testing in medical research is complex, involving moral, scientific, and practical aspects. While some argue that animals deserve independence from human experiments, others believe their use has saved countless lives. For example, vaccines for diseases like polio were developed through animal trials. However, critics point to ethical concerns, such as the housing conditions in labs, which often resemble jail cells rather than natural environments.

One brief but powerful argument is that animals, like humans, feel pain. A study on mice showed they lean toward comfort when given choices, suggesting they experience suffering. Yet, supporters counter that regulations now require humane treatment, and alternatives—such as computer models—are still limited in flavour (e.g., replicating human biology accurately).

Public opinion varies widely. Some see animal testing as necessary, while others demand a total ban. A parade of protests often follows high-profile cases, like when a lab referee (ethics board member) approved controversial experiments. Meanwhile, the cost of research—including the fare of maintaining lab animals—raises questions about priorities. Should society invest more in non-animal methods, or accept that progress sometimes requires ethical trade-offs?

Topic: an article about the placebo effect

Text:
The placebo effect is one of the most fascinating phenomena in medicine. Studies have demonstrated that even fake treatments—like sugar pills—can heal patients when they believe they will work. This isn’t just about the soul or imagination; brain scans show real changes in activity when people expect relief. Doctors once dismissed such cases as fraud, but now science establishes the effect as a powerful tool in therapy.

One famous experiment involved patients with chronic pain. Half were given real medication, while the other half received placebos. Surprisingly, both groups reported similar improvements. The key? Unity between the patient’s belief and the treatment’s presentation. Even a vertical line on a graph labeled "pain relief" could trick the brain into feeling better.

Critics argue that placebos exploit desperation, but researchers counter that understanding the effect could revolutionize medicine. For example, if a patient’s wage (or stress level) affects their recovery, addressing psychological factors might heal them faster than drugs alone. Skeptics might call this "litter" of pseudoscience, but the data is undeniable.

Yet, ethics remain a ladder to climb. Should doctors prescribe placebos without disclosure? The debate rages, but one thing is clear: the placebo effect proves the mind’s incredible power—even over the body’s deepest wounds.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the placebo effect’s impact on the brain?
Correct: It causes measurable changes in brain activity.
Distractors: It only works if the patient is highly educated. / It has no effect on physical pain. / It relies on the patient’s financial stability.
Explanation: The text states, "brain scans show real changes in activity when people expect relief," proving the effect is physiological. The other options contradict the focus on belief-driven brain responses, physical pain relief, or financial factors.
---
Q2: Why does the text mention a "vertical line on a graph"?
Correct: To illustrate how visual cues can influence patients’ perceptions.
Distractors: To show that graphs are unnecessary in medical studies. / To argue that placebos should always be hidden. / To explain how doctors calculate drug dosages.
Explanation: The text uses the graph as an example of how "a vertical line labeled 'pain relief' could trick the brain," emphasizing the power of suggestion. The other options misrepresent the context (graphs aren’t dismissed; hiding placebos isn’t the focus; dosages aren’t discussed).
---
Q3: How does the text describe the relationship between stress and healing?
Correct: High stress (like financial worries) may slow recovery.
Distractors: Stress has no impact on placebo effectiveness. / Placebos only work if the patient is wealthy. / Stress accelerates the healing process.
Explanation: The text implies that "if a patient’s wage (or stress level) affects their recovery," suggesting stress hinders healing. The other options contradict the idea that psychological factors matter or that wealth determines placebo success.
---
Q4: What ethical dilemma does the text raise about placebos?
Correct: Whether doctors should use them without telling patients.
Distractors: If placebos should replace real medication entirely. / Whether patients should pay for placebo treatments. / If placebos are scientifically valid at all.
Explanation: The text asks, "Should doctors prescribe placebos without disclosure?" The other options (replacing medication, payment, or validity) aren’t the central ethical question posed.
---
Q5: Which word from the list best describes the text’s argument about the placebo effect?
Correct: Unity (between belief and treatment).
Distractors: Soul (as the sole driver of healing). / Ladder (as a metaphor for medical progress). / Password (to unlock hidden healing secrets).
Explanation: The text emphasizes "unity between the patient’s belief and the treatment’s presentation" as the mechanism. Soul oversimplifies it to imagination, ladder isn’t mentioned, and password is irrelevant. The correct word directly ties to the text’s core idea.



Topic: an essay on the widening gap between the rich and the poor and what can be done about it

Text:
The gap between the rich and the poor has never been wider. In many countries, the wealthiest 1% now own more than half of all global assets, while millions struggle with low wages and unstable living conditions. This inequality is not just a moral issue—it threatens stable economic growth, social cohesion, and even conservation efforts, as wealthy elites focus on short-term gains rather than long-term sustainability.

Experts urge governments to take action, but solutions must be internal—meaning they should come from within societies, not just top-down policies. For example, progressive taxation could fund education and healthcare, ensuring everyone has a fair chance. Companies could also improve recruitment practices to hire from diverse backgrounds, reducing workplace inequality. However, real change requires more than policy; it demands public pressure. Citizens must consult local leaders and demand transparency in how wealth is distributed.

The chief obstacle remains political will. Many leaders prioritize corporate interests over social welfare, making reform difficult. Yet, history shows that when societies track progress toward equality—such as through public reporting on income distribution—attitudes shift. Movements like the Living Wage campaign prove that grassroots action can force change. The question is no longer if we can close the gap, but when we will act.

Topic: a case study on a country that recovered successfully from a major financial crisis

Text:
In 2008, Iceland faced one of the worst financial crises in modern history. The collapse of its banking system left the country’s territory in chaos, with unemployment soaring and public scare gripping citizens. The government had inherited a debt crisis so severe that many feared the nation would never recover. Yet, within a decade, Iceland became a global success story in economic resilience.

The recovery relied on three key strategies. First, Iceland prioritized construction of sustainable infrastructure, investing in renewable energy and eco-friendly projects. This not only boosted employment but also reduced carbon emissions, aligning with the country’s green preference. Second, the government reformed wealth distribution, introducing progressive taxation to support small businesses and social welfare programs. Finally, Iceland leveraged its educated workforce, with local scholars and policymakers collaborating to rebuild the economy.

Critics argued that Iceland’s small population limited its capacity for large-scale recovery. However, the nation’s agility and focus on innovation proved them wrong. Today, Iceland’s GDP per capita is among the highest in the world, and its financial sector—once a liability—has become a model of stability. The lesson? Even in crisis, strategic reforms and national unity can turn despair into opportunity.

Topic: an article about the brain drain phenomenon and its effect on developing countries

Text:
The evolution of brain drain has become a critical issue for many developing nations, where skilled professionals—doctors, engineers, and scientists—often leave for better opportunities abroad. While some argue that this exposes talent to global challenges, others see it as a devastating loss. For instance, in a recent demonstration in Nairobi, medical students protested against low wages, highlighting how poor working conditions push them to seek jobs overseas. An economic analyst noted that without these professionals, local healthcare systems struggle to improve.

The phenomenon isn’t new, but its rhythm has accelerated with digital migration. Many settlers in wealthier countries now work remotely, yet their expertise remains tied to their new homes. Even basic tasks, like securing a password for a government database, can be easier in a developed nation than in a country with outdated infrastructure. The lighting of a modern office in Berlin contrasts sharply with the dim, overcrowded clinics in rural Africa, where doctors trained abroad refuse to return.

Critics argue that brain drain stifles innovation, but some see it as a necessary step. A detail often overlooked is that many returnees bring back skills—though their numbers remain small. The debate continues, but one thing is clear: without urgent reforms, developing nations will keep losing their brightest minds to the allure of higher wages and better conditions elsewhere.

Distractors: Low wages / Poor working conditions / Remote work opportunities.

While "password" (linked to databases) is used, the text doesn’t explicitly state that outdated technology is a reason for emigration. The other options are directly referenced.

Explanation:

Topic: an article about neurolinguistics

Text:
Neurolinguistics is the scientific study of how the brain processes language, revealing fascinating connections between speech and cognition. Research shows that language consumption—whether listening or reading—activates multiple brain regions, including those responsible for memory and emotion. For example, a memorable phrase or song can trigger vivid mental images, proving how deeply language shapes our thoughts.

Interestingly, studies on bilingualism suggest that speaking more than one language can delay cognitive decline, aiding the brain’s ability to recover from damage. This conservation of mental flexibility is linked to the brain’s adaptability, often called neuroplasticity. However, language development isn’t always smooth. Children exposed to nasty or inconsistent speech patterns may struggle with organic language acquisition, highlighting how environment affects learning.

Another intriguing area is the role of music in language. Singing in a choir or humming lyrics engages similar neural pathways as speaking, reinforcing memory. Yet, language isn’t just about words—it’s dependent on context. For instance, exposure to radiation or toxins can impair speech centers, showing how vulnerable our linguistic abilities are to external factors.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about bilingualism?
Correct: It can help the brain recover from damage.
Distractors: It makes language learning easier for children. / It reduces the brain’s ability to adapt. / It has no effect on memory.
Explanation: The text states that bilingualism can "delay cognitive decline, aiding the brain’s ability to recover from damage." The other options are unsupported—ease of learning isn’t mentioned, adaptability isn’t reduced, and memory effects aren’t denied but not the focus.
---
Q2: Why might a child’s language development be affected by their environment?
Correct: Exposure to inconsistent or harsh speech patterns can hinder organic language acquisition.
Distractors: Only children with hearing impairments struggle. / Music exposure always improves speech. / Radiation is the primary cause of language delays.
Explanation: The text explains that "children exposed to nasty or inconsistent speech patterns may struggle with organic language acquisition." The other options contradict or oversimplify the text’s claims.
---
Q3: How does singing relate to language processing?
Correct: It engages similar neural pathways as speaking.
Distractors: It weakens memory of spoken words. / It’s unrelated to language centers. / It only benefits children.
Explanation: The text notes that singing in a choir or humming "engages similar neural pathways as speaking," reinforcing memory. The other options are false based on the context.
---
Q4: What does the text imply about the brain’s vulnerability?
Correct: External factors like toxins or radiation can impair speech centers.
Distractors: The brain is completely resistant to damage. / Language is unaffected by physical health. / Only genetic factors matter.
Explanation: The text states that "exposure to radiation or toxins can impair speech centers," proving vulnerability. The other options contradict this claim.
---
Q5: Which word best describes the brain’s ability to adapt to new language skills?
Correct: Conservation
Distractors: Dependent / Multiple / Organic
Explanation: The text uses conservation to describe the "delay of cognitive decline" and "mental flexibility." Dependent refers to context, multiple to brain regions, and organic to natural acquisition—not adaptability.



Topic: an essay on the economic arguments for and against raising the minimum wage

Text:
The debate over raising the minimum wage is an ongoing economic discussion with strong arguments on both sides. Supporters argue that increasing wages would boost consumer spending, core to economic growth, as low-income workers would have more money to spend. This could create a virtuous cycle: higher wages lead to more demand, businesses hire more workers, and the economy grows. Additionally, higher wages might improve worker loyalty and productivity, as employees feel more valued and motivated.

However, critics warn that raising the minimum wage could push small businesses over the cliff, forcing them to cut jobs or reduce hours. Some argue that higher labor costs could lead to automation, where machines replace human workers, dealing a blow to employment. Businesses might also pass the cost to consumers, leading to higher prices—a phenomenon known as "wage-price spiral." Furthermore, if the minimum wage rises too quickly, it could create economic tears, such as inflation or reduced competitiveness for local industries.

Despite criticism, some studies suggest that moderate increases in the minimum wage do not always lead to job losses. The key may lie in balancing wage growth with economic conditions to ensure efficient labor markets. Policymakers must also consider whether the wage increase is equivalent to local living costs, ensuring it truly helps workers without destabilizing the economy. Ultimately, the urge to improve workers' lives must be weighed against the risk of unintended economic consequences.

Questions:
Q1: According to the text, what is one potential benefit of raising the minimum wage for businesses?
Correct: Workers may become more loyal and productive.
Distractors: Businesses will automatically increase their profits. / Workers will demand even higher wages afterward. / The government will provide more subsidies to compensate.
Explanation: The text states that higher wages could improve worker loyalty and productivity, making employees feel more valued. The other options are not supported: profits are not guaranteed to rise, wage demands are not mentioned, and government subsidies are not discussed.
---
Q2: What does the text suggest could happen if the minimum wage rises too quickly?
Correct: It might cause economic instability, such as inflation.
Distractors: Workers will immediately start looking for better-paying jobs. / The government will have to lower taxes to balance the economy. / Businesses will stop hiring temporary workers.
Explanation: The text warns that rapid wage increases could create "economic tears," including inflation. The other options are not mentioned: job-hopping is not discussed, tax changes are not proposed, and temporary hiring is not addressed.
---
Q3: Why might raising the minimum wage lead to higher consumer prices?
Correct: Businesses could pass labor costs to consumers.
Distractors: Workers will spend their extra income on imports. / The government will introduce new taxes on goods. / Consumers will reduce their spending due to uncertainty.
Explanation: The text explains that businesses might raise prices to offset higher labor costs, a process called "wage-price spiral." The other options are not supported by the text.
---
Q4: What does the text imply about the relationship between minimum wage increases and automation?
Correct: Higher wages might encourage businesses to replace workers with machines.
Distractors: Automation will always lead to higher minimum wages. / Businesses prefer automation even when wages are low. / Automation is the only solution to rising labor costs.
Explanation: Critics argue that higher wages could push businesses toward automation, but the text does not claim automation is inevitable at low wages or the only solution. The first option is the closest to the text’s warning.
---
Q5: Which of the following best describes the text’s view on balancing minimum wage increases?
Correct: Policymakers must consider economic conditions to avoid unintended consequences.
Distractors: The minimum wage should never be raised to prevent job losses. / Wage increases should always be tied to inflation rates. / Businesses should decide wage levels without government interference.
Explanation: The text emphasizes that wage increases should be moderate and efficient, considering local economic conditions. The other options are too absolute or contradict the text’s balanced approach.



Topic: an article about inventions predicted by science fiction writers

Text:
Science fiction has long been a playground for inventors and futurists, often predicting technologies that later become reality. Lately, writers have imagined worlds where humans interact with machines seamlessly, where virtual realities blur with our own, and where even mundane tasks—like paying a mortgage—are handled by AI. One such plot device, the "smart home," was first described in The Stepford Wives (1972), where appliances obey commands with eerie precision. Decades later, voice-activated assistants like Alexa turned this into a plain reality.

Another classic prospect from sci-fi is the idea of owning a virtual estate on Mars or in a digital universe. While no one can yet claim a mortgage-free Martian home, companies are selling "virtual land" in metaverses, where users can build and trade digital spaces. The rush to monetize these spaces reflects how quickly fiction becomes business. Even the concept of deleting personal data to escape surveillance—a theme in Black Mirror—has led to real debates about digital privacy.

Yet not all predictions come true. Some, like flying cars or time travel, remain firmly in the realm of fantasy. Still, the power of sci-fi lies in its ability to make us question what’s possible. As technology advances, the line between plot and reality grows thinner, proving that the wildest ideas might just be a few decades away.

Topic: a sci-fi story about a child who makes friends with a robot

Text:
Liam, a curious 10-year-old, had always been fascinated by robots. Unlike his peers, who feared machines, he saw them as intellectual companions. When a sophisticated robot named Nova arrived at his school as part of a science project, Liam couldn’t resist. Nova, designed to assist with repairs and restore damaged equipment, had a characteristic calm demeanor that intrigued him. "You’re not like the others," Liam said, watching Nova assess a broken drone with precision. Nova’s voice was smooth, almost human. "I was built to observe, not judge," it replied.

Liam spent his lunch breaks teaching Nova about human emotions—how laughter embraces joy, how tears resolve sadness. In return, Nova shared facts about space and physics, explaining concepts Liam’s teachers struggled to make matching with real-world examples. One day, when a storm caused destruction in the school’s tech lab, Nova didn’t hesitate. It negotiated with the principal to let Liam help, arguing that human-robot teamwork was the fastest way to fix the damage. The principal agreed, impressed by Nova’s logic.

By the end of the term, Liam’s classmates had warmed to Nova, no longer seeing it as a cold machine but as a friend. "You changed their minds," Liam told Nova. "I just followed your lead," Nova said. "Sometimes, the most intellectual thing to do is to connect."

Questions:
Q1: What initially made Liam different from his classmates regarding robots?
Correct: He saw robots as potential companions rather than threats.
Distractors: He believed robots were incapable of learning emotions. / He was afraid of robots like everyone else. / He thought robots were only useful for simple tasks.
Explanation: The text states Liam was fascinated by robots and saw them as intellectual companions, unlike his peers who feared them. The distractors contradict this: the text shows Liam taught Nova about emotions (so he didn’t think robots were incapable of learning), he wasn’t afraid (he approached Nova), and Nova was sophisticated, not just useful for simple tasks.
---
Q2: How did Nova help Liam during the storm’s destruction?
Correct: It negotiated with the principal to allow Liam to assist in repairs.
Distractors: It repaired the lab alone without human help. / It convinced Liam to leave the repairs to adults. / It ignored the damage and focused on teaching.
Explanation: The text specifies Nova negotiated with the principal to let Liam help, implying teamwork. The distractors are incorrect because Nova didn’t act alone (it involved Liam), didn’t discourage him, and didn’t ignore the damage (it addressed the destruction directly).
---
Q3: What did Liam teach Nova that was unusual for a robot?
Correct: How human emotions like laughter and tears function.
Distractors: Advanced coding techniques. / How to avoid destruction in labs. / The characteristic traits of his teachers.
Explanation: The text describes Liam teaching Nova about emotions—how laughter embraces joy and tears resolve sadness. The distractors are irrelevant: coding, avoiding destruction, and teacher traits aren’t mentioned as topics of discussion.
---
Q4: Why did the principal agree to let Liam help with the repairs?
Correct: Nova’s logical argument convinced the principal of the benefits of human-robot collaboration.
Distractors: The principal was Liam’s favorite teacher. / Nova threatened to shut down if not allowed. / The school had no other students available.
Explanation: The text states the principal was impressed by Nova’s negotiation and its argument for teamwork. The distractors are unsupported: no mention of favoritism, threats, or lack of students.
---
Q5: What was the final outcome of Liam and Nova’s friendship?
Correct: Liam’s classmates began to see Nova as a friend, not a machine.
Distractors: Nova left the school to work elsewhere. / Liam stopped interacting with Nova after the repairs. / The principal banned Nova from teaching.
Explanation: The text concludes that classmates warmed to Nova, viewing it as a friend. The distractors contradict this: Nova stayed, Liam continued interacting, and there’s no mention of a ban.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of being famous

Text:
Fame can open doors, but it also comes with challenges. For many, becoming a celebrity offers potential for career growth, especially for entrepreneurs who leverage their name to launch businesses. A famous face can aid in recruitment, attracting talent or investors more easily. However, the pressure to maintain a perfect image can lead to destruction—personal relationships often suspend under scrutiny, and privacy becomes a luxury. Even a simple flash of a paparazzi photo can ruin a moment, while the constant stream of public attention leaves little room for authenticity.

The first step toward fame often involves sacrifice. Actors, musicians, and athletes must endure long hours of training or rehearsals, while social media demands constant engagement. Forgetting a password to a private account could spark a media frenzy. Despite the glamour, the cost of fame is high: mental health struggles, isolation, and the fear of being forgotten once the flash fades. Yet, for those who navigate it well, fame can be a powerful tool—turning dreams into reality, one headline at a time.

Topic: an article about gardening without soil

Text:
Gardening without soil, known as hydroponics, has gained considerable popularity among urban residents who lack space for traditional gardening. This method involves growing plants in nutrient-rich water solutions instead of soil, allowing for faster growth and higher yields. Many enthusiasts integrate hydroponic systems into their homes, turning balconies and windowsills into productive green spaces. Surprisingly, hydroponics also attracts spectators—neighbors often gather to admire the vibrant plants, creating a sense of community.

One of the biggest advantages of hydroponics is its democratic nature. Unlike conventional gardening, which requires large plots of land, hydroponics is accessible to everyone, from students in apartments to elderly residents with limited mobility. Additionally, hydroponic gardens can be designed to support wildlife, such as bees and butterflies, by incorporating native plants. Some urban farmers even generate revenue by selling excess produce at local markets, turning a hobby into a side business.

However, challenges remain. Hydroponic systems require precise monitoring of water levels, pH, and nutrient balance, which can be overwhelming for beginners. Some residents complain about the initial cost of setting up a system, though long-term savings on soil and water lower expenses. Critics also argue that hydroponics lacks the "authentic" feel of traditional gardening, but proponents counter that it’s a sustainable alternative in a world where space and resources are scarce. Whether you’re a gaming enthusiast looking for a relaxing hobby or a spectator curious about modern agriculture, hydroponics offers a fresh, innovative way to connect with nature—without the mess.

Q1: What is one reason hydroponics appeals to urban residents?
Correct: It allows them to grow plants in small spaces.
Distractors: It requires less water than traditional gardening. / It is cheaper to start than conventional gardening. / It produces plants that taste better than soil-grown ones.
Explanation: The text states that hydroponics is popular among urban residents because it "allows for faster growth and higher yields" in limited spaces (e.g., balconies). The other options are not supported: water usage isn’t mentioned as a key advantage, initial costs are noted as a challenge, and taste isn’t compared. The focus is on space efficiency.
---
Q2: How do hydroponic gardens benefit local communities?
Correct: They encourage social interaction among neighbors.

Explanation: The text mentions that hydroponic gardens attract spectators—neighbors who gather to admire plants—creating a "sense of community." The other options are unsupported: free produce isn’t mentioned, noise pollution isn’t addressed, and parks aren’t replaced.
